## §KYA-05 — Vignette Cycle 5: "The Princess of Zábulistán"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycles 1 and 3 dealt with what the Shah-Nameh includes. Cycle 5 deals with what it omits. Ferdowsi's text mentions Jemshíd's consort only obliquely — she took poison when he was captured. Her name is not given. A 10th-century court history of Zábulistán gives her name and her account of Jemshíd's last years. The archive's commission is not to correct the epic but to hold what the epic did not carry.

### TOKEN: "The Zábulistán Chronicle"

Four folios of a 10th-century court history of Zábulistán, copied in the 12th century from the original palace records. The text gives the queen's name as Sháhdokht and records her account of Jemshíd's deterioration: "He stopped asking for counsel and began demanding confirmation. God's blessing is not withdrawn — it is simply no longer sought." She chose poison when the capital fell; the chronicle names the day, the preparation, and who was present. Heavy parchment, bound between carved wooden boards.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "zabustan_chronicle"
- `name`: "The Zábulistán Chronicle"
- `description`: "Four folios naming Jemshíd's queen Sháhdokht, her account of his last years, and her death by poison when the capital fell. 'He stopped asking for counsel and began demanding confirmation.' The Shah-Nameh does not give her name. This chronicle does."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**New nodes required:**
- `KBL` — Kabul Mountain Fortress District (near ancient Zábulistán; the Hindu Kush foothills)

**Existing nodes used:**
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`KBL` (Kabul — court historian Malikshah al-Sultani) → `TAB` (Tabriz — historian Ahmad al-Farghani) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Name in the Court History" (Kabul)**

Malikshah al-Sultani is perhaps seventy-five. He has held the Zábulistán Chronicle in his library for forty years and has been trying to decide what to do with it for the last ten. The political situation in Kabul has become untenable — Tughlaq pressure from the east, Timurid scouts to the northwest. He does not trust any local institution.

He places the four folios on the reading stand. "The Shah-Nameh says nothing about her name. It says only that she took poison. This chronicle says her name was Sháhdokht. It also says what she noticed about him in the final years." He waits.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince him that the Weimar archive is a destination that does not require the document to resolve the Shah-Nameh's omission one way or another; the archive holds the supplement beside the epic without preferring either.*

*On success:* He nods. "A supplement beside the text. That is the correct relationship." He wraps the folios in the carved-board binding himself and hands them to you.

*On fail:* He is uncertain. He wants to know more about the archive's classification practices before releasing the document to a stranger. Investigation DC 11 to describe the archive's existing category system in credible detail.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Zábulistán Chronicle received in carved wooden boards.*

---

**ACT II — "The Compromised Translator" (road from Kabul west)**

You have hired a local translator for the road through the mountain passes. Three days out, at a caravanserai, you notice him studying the chronicles' carved boards closely — examining the binding's decoration, not reading the text.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — determine whether the translator has been commissioned to identify and switch the chronicle for a blank folio at the earliest opportunity.*

*On success:* You catch him speaking quietly with a rider at the caravanserai — a man who arrived after you but took a table within earshot. You keep the chronicle on your person for the rest of the road and release the translator at the next junction.

*On fail:* The switch is made that night. You discover the blank folio at the next caravanserai. You must trace the translator's contact — an additional day's effort — before recovering the real chronicle.

*TOKEN check Act II — chronicle intact if Insight succeeds; one-day delay and one additional skill check (Athletics DC 11) if it fails.*

---

**ACT III — "The Shah-Nameh Purist" (Tabriz)**

Ahmad al-Farghani receives the chronicle with the quiet excitement of a man who has been expecting something like this for decades. He reads it carefully. When he reaches Sháhdokht's account of Jemshíd's final years, he reads it twice.

"She says: 'He stopped asking for counsel and began demanding confirmation.' Ferdowsi says: 'God withdrew his blessing.' These are the same thing, but from different distances. She was in the room."

The problem: a Khorasani copyist working in Tabriz is known for supplementing manuscripts before they leave the city — adding his own harmonizing notes that smooth over discrepancies between texts. He has already heard the chronicle has arrived. He will request access through al-Farghani's scholarly network.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — decline the copyist's access request before he can add harmonizing notes to the chronicle. The chronicle's value is precisely that it doesn't harmonize; a gloss that explains away the discrepancy removes the evidence.*

*On success:* Al-Farghani supports the refusal. The copyist withdraws. The chronicle leaves Tabriz without unauthorized additions.

*On fail:* The copyist adds a marginal note: "The queen's account and the Shah-Nameh account are reconcilable as follows—" Sweelinck will note in the intake record that the marginal addition is secondary and does not reflect the chronicle's original content.

*TOKEN check Act III — al-Farghani adds his own authentication note.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Collector's Formal Offer" (road from Tabriz north)**

A professional manuscript dealer traveling north makes you a formal offer. His client is a collector of Sassanian-era court histories and has authorized a purchase price that is genuinely fair — not an insult, not a trap. The dealer has a Venetian commercial letter of authentication.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 13 — decline a legitimate commercial offer without making an enemy. The document has been designated for a specific archive; commercial designation cannot supersede a delivery commission.*

*On success:* He accepts the decline with professional courtesy and notes the exchange in his ledger.

*On fail:* He follows at a discreet distance for two days, waiting for a moment when the designation can be reconsidered. No combat — he is a dealer, not a thug. But he makes one more offer at the final junction before Weimar's territory.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Name That Was Always There" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the four folios slowly. When he reaches Sháhdokht's account of Jemshíd's deterioration, he sets the folio down and reads it again.

"'He stopped asking for counsel and began demanding confirmation.' This is a precise account of how a king loses the gift of just rule. Ferdowsi describes the result. She describes the process." He looks at the carved wooden boards. "She was in the room. The archive will note that."

He writes in the master ledger: *Shah-Nameh Supplementary Records — Unnamed Women, Volume I. Name: Sháhdokht, consort of Jemshíd, Zábulistán. Source: Zábulistán court chronicle, 10th century, Kabul copy, 12th century. The Shah-Nameh does not give her name. This document does.*

"The supplement does not correct the epic. It stands beside it. That is the correct relationship."

*TOKEN taken Act V — Zábulistán Chronicle filed as first entry in a new series.*

---

### Theme

**"The woman whose name Ferdowsi left blank; the court chronicle that filled the gap without correcting the epic; the account of the man who stopped asking and started demanding, from someone who was in the room when it happened; the archive creates Shah-Nameh Supplementary Records — Unnamed Women, Volume I, because she was always there."**

---

## §KYA-06 — Vignette Cycle 6: "The Record of Successive Honors"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 1 documented the banner's origin — Kavah's leather apron as the emblem of legitimate rule. Cycle 6 documents what came after: every Sassanian king, for a thousand years, added gold and gemstones to the blacksmith's apron. A metalworkers' guild kept the ledger of each addition because guilds understand ongoing debts. The banner was destroyed in 651 AD when the Arabs conquered Persia. The ledger survived.

### TOKEN: "The Guild Ledger"

Seventeen folios of the Anjuman-i Zargar (Tabriz Goldsmith's Brotherhood), 6th-10th century, listing each Sassanian king who added to the Derafsh Kaviani: the materials added, the craftsman's name, the occasion. Each king's entry opens with a formulaic phrase: *"added in acknowledgment of the banner's origin"* — the same phrase, in slightly different Persian dialects, from six dynasties. The ledger is written in a professional craft hand, not a literary one. Bound in the guild's brown leather.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "guild_ledger"
- `name`: "The Guild Ledger"
- `description`: "Seventeen folios recording each Sassanian king's addition to the Derafsh Kaviani, 6th-10th century. Six dynasties, same opening phrase: 'added in acknowledgment of the banner's origin.' They knew what they were venerating and they said so every time. The banner is gone. The record of every gift to it survives."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `RGS` — Ragusa (Dubrovnik) Dalmatian Republic
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`TAB` (Tabriz — Anjuman-i Zargar guild master Khalid ibn Ahmad al-Zargar) → `RGS` (Ragusa waystation) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Dying Guild Master" (Tabriz, Anjuman-i Zargar guild house)**

Khalid ibn Ahmad al-Zargar is the guild master and is dying — not dramatically, but steadily, and he knows it. The Anjuman-i Zargar has kept the Guild Ledger since it arrived in Tabriz from the Sassanian archive after the Arab conquest. Most guild members do not know what it is. The incoming guild master, his nephew, is a competent craftsman and an incurious man who would keep it in the archive the same way you keep anything whose function you don't understand — stored, not read.

Khalid places the ledger on the worktable. "Every king who added to Kavah's apron acknowledged in the entry that it was Kavah's apron. That phrase is in there six times, from six different kings who couldn't have copied each other directly. They understood what they were venerating." He is quiet for a moment. "Take it somewhere that will also understand."

*Skill check: History DC 12 — recognize the specific significance: six dynasties used the same phrase about a craftsman's tool without any single canonical source requiring them to do so; this is independent corroboration that the banner's origin-meaning was understood and maintained.*

*On success:* He tells you the guild's authentication seal is pressed into the back cover, certifying the folios as the original record. He adds his own personal attestation: *"Final custody: Khalid ibn Ahmad al-Zargar, 1367. Destination: Archivus Sweelinck, Weimar."*

*On fail:* He hands the ledger without explanation. You learn its significance from reading it on the road.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Guild Ledger received in brown guild leather.*

---

**ACT II — "The Ottoman Collector on the Road" (road from Tabriz toward Ragusa)**

A Turkish merchant prince traveling west at a caravanserai. He collects Sassanian historical materials and has heard through the Tabriz trade network that the Anjuman-i Zargar has released its primary historical document. He introduces himself correctly and makes an offer that is neither insulting nor coercive.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — decline without revealing the destination. "The item has been designated for a specific institution" is the correct framing; naming the institution would invite a competing offer to the institution.*

*On success:* He accepts the refusal gracefully and presents a formal receipt of first-refusal if the designation falls through. You pocket the receipt as a courtesy.

*On fail:* He becomes curious about the destination and presses. His curiosity is not threatening but adds three hours to your roadside conversation while he makes the case for his collection's importance.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Florence Consignment" (Ragusa waystation inn)**

The Ragusa inns serve as the last Mediterranean waystation before the northern road. A Genoese manuscript dealer at the main inn has a consignment going to Florence — twelve items, a courier leaving in two days. He spots the guild leather on your pack and asks: "That's a professional binding. How old is it?"

He is not hostile. He is simply excellent at his job, which involves identifying materials before anyone tells him to stop.

*Skill check: Insight or Deception DC 12 — determine whether the dealer has already identified the ledger's origin (he has not; he is guessing from the leather quality), and either confirm or redirect without naming it.*

*On success:* You redirect his interest toward a general-commerce framing. He does not add the ledger to the Florence consignment and loses interest before you finish your meal.

*On fail:* He sends a description of the binding to a Florence correspondent for identification. No direct threat, but a Florence collector will be waiting at a later junction.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "The False Chain-of-Custody" (northern road through Hungary)**

A well-dressed agent presents a letter claiming the ledger is the property of a Tabriz family who are seeking its return through commercial channels. The letter names the family: the Rashidi household, descendants of a Persian court official.

*Skill check: Investigation DC 12 — the Anjuman-i Zargar's own ledger entries would show that the ledger has been in guild custody since 651 AD, significantly predating any Rashidi family's possible connection to it. The family name does not appear in the ledger's chain-of-custody at any point.*

*On success:* The agent cannot produce a guild document supporting the claim. You point out the discrepancy. He withdraws.

*On fail:* He has a secondary document — a 13th-century notation that may or may not be authentic. The dispute goes to a local roadside magistrate. Persuasion DC 12 at the magistrate's table.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "What the Kings Were Paying For" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the ledger entry by entry. He spends a long time on the formulaic phrase. He reads it in six different hands, from six different centuries.

"'Added in acknowledgment of the banner's origin.' Six times. From six dynasties. Not one of them was required to use this phrase by any canonical text — there is no liturgical formula that demands it. Each king wrote it separately. Each king knew what they were venerating."

He closes the ledger gently.

"They were paying an ongoing debt to a blacksmith. Every decoration was a new acknowledgment that the banner came from a craftsman's work-apron and that the debt was not discharged by venerating it once. The guild kept this record because guilds understand that debts of this kind compound with honor rather than diminishing with interest."

He writes in the master ledger: *Derafsh Kaviani Material Records — Guild Ledger of Successive Royal Additions. Final custody: Anjuman-i Zargar, Tabriz, established 651 AD. The banner is destroyed. The record of what was given to it, and what was acknowledged each time, survives.*

*TOKEN taken Act V — Guild Ledger archived.*

---

### Theme

**"The banner does not survive but the guild record does; six dynasties used the same phrase about a craftsman's tool without being required to by any canon; they were paying an ongoing debt to Kavah that no decoration could discharge; the archive holds the debt-register for an object that no longer exists."**

---

## §KYA-07 — Vignette Cycle 7: "The Símúrgh's Second Feather"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

The Shah-Nameh says the Símúrgh gave Zál a feather and that Zál burned it when Rustem's birth required divine intervention. The poem implies this was the only feather. But the Atkinson text says "a feather from his wing" — possibly meaning one of several. A Yazd fire temple's inventory records a sealed ivory case: "one feather, origin: Alberz mountain, provenance: Zál son of Sám, given to the second keeper by the first keeper's son." Cycle 7 follows the case that the poem forgot to mention.

### TOKEN: "The Sealed Ivory Case"

A palm-sized ivory case, locked with a simple bronze pin, sealed at the join with the fire temple's wax mark. The inventory text is painted on the outside in small red script. The case has not been opened since it arrived in the 11th century. Whether the interior contains a Símúrgh feather or a hawk's feather from the same mountain is unknown. The inventory does not claim supernatural origin — only the chain of custody.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "sealed_ivory_case"
- `name`: "The Sealed Ivory Case"
- `description`: "Palm-sized ivory case, locked, sealed with the Yazd fire temple's mark. Inventory text in red: 'one feather, origin: Alberz mountain, provenance: Zál son of Sám, given to the second keeper by the first keeper's son.' Not opened since the 11th century. The inventory makes no supernatural claim."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `YAZ` — Yazd Fire Temple Quarter (established in KYA-03)
- `CON` — Constantinople Byzantine Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`YAZ` (Yazd fire temple — fire-keeper Ardeshir) → `CON` (Constantinople — paleographer Nikephoros Katakalon) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Second Entry in the Temple Inventory" (Yazd fire temple)**

Ardeshir is the junior fire-keeper — younger than Bahram, precise where Bahram was contemplative. He has been the temple's inventory officer for twenty years and has known about the sealed ivory case since his first audit. He has never opened it.

"We know what the inventory says. We don't know what is inside. Opening it would answer the question and end it. Leaving it sealed means the question keeps existing." He pauses. "We are not equipped to answer it correctly. Whatever is inside, we would assign the wrong meaning. Take it to someone who can hold the question without answering it."

The waqf dispute from KYA-03 is in the background — Ardeshir wants the case gone before it becomes exhibit evidence in a property hearing.

*Skill check: History DC 11 — demonstrate understanding of what the Símúrgh feather means in the Shah-Nameh tradition and why the inventory's chain-of-custody claim is worth preserving whether or not the feather is supernatural.*

*On success:* He seals the bronze pin with a fresh wax impression of the temple mark and writes a transfer note on a slip of paper tucked into the pin-hole. "Chain of custody: Yazd fire temple, 11th century to 1367. Transferred to bearer for permanent archive custody."

*On fail:* He hands the case without the transfer note. You have the case but not the formal chain-of-custody documentation.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Sealed Ivory Case received, bronze pin sealed, transfer note inside.*

---

**ACT II — "The Physician's Curiosity" (road from Yazd toward Constantinople)**

A Greek physician from Constantinople is on the same road, heading south for medical work in Isfahan. He has heard about the case through the Yazd community — he is part of a natural history circle in Constantinople and has been hoping to examine what might be a rare bird specimen.

He makes a polite request. He is curious, not grasping. He genuinely believes scientific examination serves scholarship better than continued ignorance.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — decline the examination request without making an enemy. The case's value to the archive is precisely its sealed, unexamined state; scientific examination would resolve one question by eliminating another.*

*On success:* He is gracious and asks only whether you will allow him to report the case's existence to the Constantinople scholarly circle when he returns. You agree to that part.

*On fail:* He is mildly offended and reports to the Constantinople circle with a note that the carrier was hostile to natural history. Katakalon receives this impression before your arrival.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Question of Whether to Open It" (Constantinople)**

Nikephoros Katakalon has assembled three colleagues from the natural history circle. Two want to open the case. One — Katakalon — does not. He has been expecting this argument and has prepared a position: the scholarly value of an unopened case lies precisely in the question it contains; examining the contents resolves the inventory claim but also destroys the archival interest.

His two colleagues disagree, not unreasonably.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 13 — support Katakalon's position in the three-person debate. The argument: the archive that receives a sealed case with a credible inventory record holds two things — the inventory claim and the unexamined reality; both are preserved; opening the case reduces the archive's holdings by one.*

*On success:* Katakalon wins the debate with your support. The case remains sealed. He adds a notation to the exterior in Greek: *"Received sealed, 1367, inventory verified without opening. The sealed state is the primary archival condition. N. Katakalon, Constantinople."*

*On fail:* One of the colleagues opens the case before you can prevent it. Inside: a single iridescent feather, three inches long, unusual coloring. Not definitively supernatural. The colleague adds a notation: *"Opened in Constantinople: one feather, iridescent. Origin undetermined."* The case is resealed. Sweelinck will receive an opened case rather than a sealed one.

*TOKEN check Act III — case sealed or opened, notation added either way.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Collector Who Heard About the Feather" (road north)**

Word has traveled faster than you have. A Venetian natural history collector has a representative on the northern road with an offer — the representative is polite, the offer is substantial, and behind him at a discreet distance are four hired riders who will help if polite fails.

*Skill check: Athletics DC 11 — the hired riders are waiting at the road junction ahead; turn south on the secondary track before reaching the junction, adding half a day's travel but avoiding the interception point.*

*On success:* The secondary track works. The hired riders wait at the wrong junction until dark.

*On fail:* The hired riders reach you before the secondary track. Combat: 4 opponents, AC 11, HP 16 each. The ivory case is in your pack and is not damaged in the fight.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Archive That Will Not Open It Either" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck receives the sealed case (or the reopened-and-resealed case, if Act III failed). He reads the inventory text on the exterior, the transfer note inside the pin-hole, and Katakalon's notation.

He does not open it.

"The inventory says: one feather, origin Alberz mountain, provenance Zál son of Sám. That is the inventory's claim. Whether the content matches the claim can only be determined by opening the case and losing the question. I am not prepared to lose the question."

He sets the case in a sealed section and writes in the master ledger: *Precautionary Objects — Shah-Nameh, Zál and Símúrgh connection. Condition: sealed. Inventory claim: one feather, Alberz mountain provenance. Contents: not verified. The sealed state is the primary archival condition.*

Then, after a pause: "If the Símúrgh gave Zál more than one feather, this was the second. The first was burned at need. The second was never needed. The precaution that was never used is still a precaution. That is its own kind of fact."

*TOKEN taken Act V — Sealed Ivory Case archived in sealed condition.*

---

### Theme

**"The precaution the poem forgot to mention; the second feather exists because the first one was burned; the question of whether it is truly the Símúrgh's is preserved by the archive remaining sealed; the precaution that was never used is still a precaution — that is its own kind of fact."**

---

## §KYA-08 — Vignette Cycle 8: "The Caravanserai Account"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 1 documented the Shah-Nameh's founding myth from the inside — the craftsman's act, the banner raised, the poem's political charge. Cycle 8 looks at the same tradition from the completely outside: a caravanserai account of the night Rustem passed through disguised as a salt merchant. The army's record calls it a brilliant stratagem. The innkeeper's account says a hundred camels arrived at sundown, they paid in salt, and they were quiet.

### TOKEN: "The Inn Record"

Six folios of a caravanserai account, the 22nd copy in an unbroken generational line stretching from the original innkeeper's night-log to the current Hasan ibn Rustam al-Sistani, 33rd generation. Each generation added one line to the cover: a summary of the transmission. The original night-log entry (reconstructed) reads: *"One hundred camels. Salt. Payment exact. Men quiet. Departed before first light. No trouble."* Below this, over twenty-two copies, twenty-two one-line comments from successive keepers: variants on "it is said our ancestor served Rustem that night." Bound in caravanserai brown leather.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "inn_record"
- `name`: "The Inn Record"
- `description`: "Six folios, 22nd copy in a generational line from the original innkeeper's night-log. 'One hundred camels. Salt. Payment exact. Men quiet.' Twenty-two successive keepers each added one line: variants on 'it is said our ancestor served Rustem that night.' The army's account does not mention the salt-payment. The inn record does not mention the demon-fortress."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**New nodes required:**
- `SIS` — Sístán Province Road Town (the hero's homeland, eastern Persia)

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SIS` (Sístán — innkeeper Hasan ibn Rustam al-Sistani, 33rd generation) → `SAM` (Samarkand — merchant-scholar Ahmad al-Tirmidhi) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Thirty-Third Generation" (Sístán, caravanserai road town)**

Hasan ibn Rustam al-Sistani runs the family caravanserai. He is the 33rd generation. He made the 22nd copy himself twenty years ago, in his own hand, and added his generation's line to the cover: *"It is said our ancestor served Rustem that night, and that the camels' loads were heavier when they left than when they arrived."*

The political situation in eastern Persia is deteriorating. Timurid advance forces have been moving through Khorasan; Sístán feels exposed. Hasan is not certain his children will maintain the account. He wants it somewhere that doesn't depend on one family's survival.

He is also considering burning it. He is not dramatic about this — he simply does not want the account used as a trophy by conquerors who will say "we have Rustem's innkeeper's family document."

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince Hasan to entrust it rather than burn it: a trophy seized by conquerors is still a document in an archive somewhere, but a burned document is gone; the archive he is sending it to has no faction.*

*On success:* He hands it over with a short statement written on the inside cover: "Released to bearer for permanent custody, by the 33rd generation. Hasan ibn Rustam al-Sistani, 1367, Sístán."

*On fail:* He gives you three days to make the argument. You stay three days and he eventually agrees, but the Timurid advance scouts pass through on day two, creating urgency.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Inn Record received, 33rd-generation release statement inside.*

---

**ACT II — "The Military Checkpoint" (road from Sístán toward Samarkand)**

Timurid advance-force soldiers at a road checkpoint. They are conducting a document inventory — confiscating written materials that might contain road or garrison intelligence. Their officer is methodical.

The Inn Record looks like a strategic document — it documents road use, trade volumes, and movement patterns across three generations.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — determine the correct framing before speaking. The document's generational one-liners are the key: it is a folklore transmission, not a road census. The officer will recognize the difference if it is pointed out correctly.*

*On success:* You show the officer the generational cover-lines. He reads them and makes the determination himself: "This is a family story, not intelligence." He passes you through.

*On fail:* He confiscates the document for senior review. It is returned two days later — the senior officer read the generational lines and agreed with the soldier's intuition. Slight damage to the outer leather binding from handling.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Compilationist's Offer" (Samarkand)**

Ahmad al-Tirmidhi is a merchant-scholar compiling "military stratagems as told by non-combatants" — a genuine scholarly project. He has been collecting civilian accounts of famous battles and maneuvers for six years. The Inn Record is exactly what he has been looking for and he becomes visibly excited.

He wants the original in his compilation. His Venetian buyer (already paid half) expects an original, not a copy.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — determine whether al-Tirmidhi would honor an agreement to copy the original for his compilation while releasing the original. He would; he is a scholar who knows the difference between a copy and an original, and a scholar who values the original's chain-of-custody.*

*On success:* You propose: the compilation receives a copy made by al-Tirmidhi's own hand, certified as a true copy with his seal. He agrees. A copy is made in two hours. *The Inn Record is authenticated by al-Tirmidhi and released.*

*On fail:* Al-Tirmidhi's Venetian buyer agent, who happens to be in Samarkand, intercedes and attempts to claim the original under the purchase agreement. Persuasion DC 12 to establish that a purchase agreement for "materials" does not supersede a prior carrier's commission.

*TOKEN check Act III — al-Tirmidhi adds his authentication note.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Road to Weimar" (road north from Samarkand)**

No single dramatic obstacle on this road — instead, a series of three minor checks at different caravanserais. A Mongol trade-route administrator wants to tax commercial manuscripts. A Byzantine road-watch official wants to inspect the contents of sealed document cases. A local lord's man wants to verify the document is not a military survey.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — at the most challenging of the three stops, provide a clear account of the document's nature: a family caravanserai tradition, not a commercial manuscript, a military survey, or a sealed document (it is open-bound leather).*

*On success:* All three stops pass without seizure.

*On fail:* One of the three stops results in a three-day hold pending review. The document is returned undamaged.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Two Accounts of the Same Night" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the Inn Record line by line. He reads the twenty-two generational additions at the front, then the body. He is quiet.

"The army's account of this stratagem describes the demon-fortress's fall, the capture of the garrison commander, the night attack. It does not mention the salt-payment." He looks up. "This account describes the salt-payment, the exact number of camels, the fact that the men were quiet. It does not mention the demon-fortress." He pauses. "Neither account is wrong. Both are primary. They describe the same night from the positions their authors occupied."

He writes in the master ledger: *Civilian Witness Records — Military Events; the account that does not know it is witnessing history. First entry in this series. Inn Record, Sístán, 33rd generation of the family of Rustem's innkeeper.*

*TOKEN taken Act V — Inn Record archived under new category.*

---

### Theme

**"The military stratagem from the innkeeper's side; the army's account describes the tactical brilliance; the caravanserai account describes the salt-payment; neither is wrong and neither references the other; the archive creates Civilian Witness Records — Military Events, for the account that does not know it is witnessing history."**

---

## §KYA-09 — Vignette Cycle 9: "The Trophy of the Broken Girdle"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 8 looked at a famous victory from the civilian side. Cycle 9 looks at the moment the Shah-Nameh's central war almost ended — Rustem grabbed Afrásiyáb by the girdle and had him. The golden belt broke. Afrásiyáb fell free. The war continued for the entire epic because this moment failed. Rustem kept the broken belt piece. An inscription on his trophy-chest at Sístán reads: "The girdle of Afrásiyáb, broken, kept as proof that Afrásiyáb was in Rustem's grip once and the world did not end."

### TOKEN: "The Shrine Rubbing"

A rubbing on heavy vellum made by the antiquarian Mahmud al-Shushtari in 1301, from the stone inscription on Rustem's trophy-chest at a shrine in Sístán. The shrine has been ruined since the Mongol destruction. The rubbing is the only record of the inscription. The ink is charcoal-grey, the vellum heavy, the rubbing preserving the stone's surface texture.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "shrine_rubbing"
- `name`: "The Shrine Rubbing"
- `description`: "Vellum rubbing of the inscription on Rustem's trophy-chest, Sístán shrine, made 1301 by Mahmud al-Shushtari. The shrine is destroyed. The rubbing survives. The inscription includes: 'The girdle of Afrásiyáb, broken, kept as proof that Afrásiyáb was in Rustem's grip once and the world did not end.'"
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SIS` — Sístán Province Road Town (established in KYA-08)
- `MRG` — Maragha Observatory District and Road Junction
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SIS` (Sístán — Farrukh al-Shushtari, grandson of the antiquarian) → `MRG` (Maragha — observatory district road junction) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Grandson's Decision" (Sístán)**

Farrukh al-Shushtari, the grandson of Mahmud al-Shushtari who made the rubbing in 1301. The rubbing has been in the family's library for sixty-six years. The shrine it records has been gone for a century — Mongol destruction. The rubbing is the only record of the inscription.

Farrukh is practical. He knows what he has. He wants a fair price (not the rubbing's maximum value — its maximum value is incalculable and he knows that too) and he wants it somewhere it will be used, not stored in a collector's cedar chest.

*Skill check: History DC 11 — recognize the specific importance of the inscription's formulation: "kept as proof that Afrásiyáb was in Rustem's grip once" is an extraordinary archival statement — it treats a near-kill as a trophy in its own right, and asks what "proof" means for a thing that almost happened.*

*On success:* Farrukh is pleased by the recognition. He names a fair price and adds a note on the reverse of the vellum: "Rubbing made by Mahmud al-Shushtari, 1301. Shrine: destroyed. This rubbing is the only record of this inscription. F. al-Shushtari, 1367."

*On fail:* He provides the rubbing for the fair price without additional documentation. The chain-of-custody is thinner.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Shrine Rubbing received, vellum reverse annotated.*

---

**ACT II — "The Collector's Interest" (road from Sístán toward Maragha)**

A collector of Sassanian-era relics and documents is on the same road, heading west. He has been collecting for twenty years and knows what is being moved through the Sístán region. He makes a polite inquiry at a caravanserai, then a polite offer.

He is not threatening. He is genuinely interested in the material, and his offer is reasonable. His collection is in Tabriz, well-maintained, accessible to scholars.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — decline while acknowledging the collection's legitimacy. The difference: a collector's Tabriz archive can be dispersed or lost; the Weimar archive has a specific institutional commitment to perpetual custody.*

*On success:* He accepts the distinction with some regret and asks you to tell Archivus Sweelinck that his Tabriz collection exists and is available for comparative work.

*On fail:* He follows to Maragha and makes a second offer there. No threat — two offers, both declined.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Astronomer's Pahlavi Question" (Maragha)**

The observatory district at Maragha is a road junction. The astronomer Mahmud al-Ghazawi, working in the observatory's adjacent library, has heard about the rubbing and asks to see it. He is interested in a specific Pahlavi phrase in the inscription — one that appears in no other document he has catalogued.

He wants the rubbing for two days to copy the phrase and cross-reference it.

*Skill check: Investigation DC 12 — determine whether al-Ghazawi has contacted the Tabriz collector (the collector from Act II follows a different road south; there is no connection between them) or whether his interest is genuine.*

*On success:* His interest is genuine and his Pahlavi expertise is real. You allow him one day to make a note. He confirms the phrase is unusual and writes his observation on a separate sheet — not on the rubbing itself.

*On fail:* You are suspicious without cause; al-Ghazawi is offended and you lose his cooperation. His Pahlavi observation is lost.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Stream Crossing" (northern road, spring flood)**

A stream on the northern road has flooded. The ford is passable but deeper than expected. The vellum rubbing cannot get wet without losing detail in the charcoal impressions.

*Skill check: Athletics DC 12 — cross the stream while keeping the rubbing dry, holding it above your head or in a sealed document case.*

*On success:* The rubbing crosses the ford dry. *No damage.*

*On fail:* One edge of the vellum is wet. The charcoal detail on that edge blurs slightly. Sweelinck will note it in the condition report.

*TOKEN check Act IV — rubbing intact or edge-damaged.*

---

**ACT V — "The Evidence of the Grip" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the rubbing carefully, tracing the stone-surface impression with one finger.

He stops at the trophy entry for the broken girdle. He reads it three times.

"'Kept as proof that Afrásiyáb was in Rustem's grip once and the world did not end.' This inscription was written by someone who kept the broken piece specifically as proof of an approach to a victory — not the victory itself. The trophy is not of the kill. The trophy is of the grip." He looks up. "I do not know of another trophy inscription that treats a near-kill as a trophy in its own right. The near-kill was what mattered, because it meant the man capable of it was real."

He writes in the master ledger: *Material Witness Records — Combat Trophies; the documentation of a grip without a kill. First entry. The broken girdle of Afrásiyáb, as described in the Sístán shrine inscription, 6th century AD. Record: Shrine Rubbing, Mahmud al-Shushtari, 1301.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The trophy that proves a grip without a kill; the inscription that treats a near-kill as a trophy in its own right; what Rustem kept proves not that he won that battle but that the man capable of winning it was real; the archive creates Material Witness Records — Combat Trophies, for the evidence of what almost happened."**

---

## §KYA-10 — Vignette Cycle 10: "The Second Bracelet"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 2 looked at the legal custom that might have saved Rustem's reputation at the cost of his son's life — the Two-Falls question. Cycle 10 looks at a quieter, harder fact: after Sohráb died, Rustem had a second bracelet in his armory. An armory inventory entry from Sístán reads: *"One gold bracelet, kept in memory of the one given away. Engraved: 'the twin of the one at Samengán.'"* The armory clerk wrote "in memory" without being asked to. The archive files it under Grief Objects.

### TOKEN: "The Weapons Chronicle Folio"

A single folio detached from a larger weapons inventory of Rustem's armory at Sístán (7th century, Arabic, with Persian marginalia), listing Rustem's personal items. Folio 47. Entry 47: "One gold bracelet. No combat use. No gemstones. Engraved: 'the twin of the one at Samengán.' Kept in memory of the one given away." The folio is loose-bound in a protective leather sleeve.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "weapons_chronicle_folio"
- `name`: "The Weapons Chronicle Folio"
- `description`: "Folio 47 of Rustem's Sístán armory inventory, 7th century. Entry 47: 'One gold bracelet. No combat use. Engraved: the twin of the one at Samengán. Kept in memory of the one given away.' The armory clerk wrote 'in memory' without being asked to."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SIS` — Sístán Province Road Town
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SIS` (Sístán — Ali ibn Dawud al-Sistani, Sístáni noble house) → `TAB` (Tabriz — Ahmad al-Farghani) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Armory Register" (Sístán)**

Ali ibn Dawud al-Sistani comes from the noble family that has held the armory inventory as a genealogical record of Sístán's heroic heritage for three hundred years. He is practical. He has already sold several folios to Venetian collectors and is not sentimental about the enterprise.

Folio 47 is still in his possession. He places it on the table.

You read Entry 47. The armory clerk wrote "kept in memory of the one given away." The phrase is not required by inventory format — all other entries are purely technical. This clerk chose to name the grief.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — establish that the Weimar archive is a better destination than a Venetian collector's cedar chest, and negotiate a fair price.*

*On success:* Ali agrees. He adds the folio to a brief document of sale that names the Weimar archive as the receiving institution. "The collector who bought Folio 12 has never looked at it again," he says. "I want this one read."

*On fail:* He has a standing offer from the Venetian collector who bought previous folios. You must match the offer price or make the argument on principle alone. Persuasion DC 12 at a second attempt.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Weapons Chronicle Folio 47, document of sale inside the sleeve.*

---

**ACT II — "The Prior Option Letter" (road north from Sístán)**

A Venetian commercial agent overtakes you at the first significant caravanserai. He has a prior option letter from Ali ibn Dawud — signed before your visit — granting the Venetian collector first right of refusal on any folio from the armory inventory.

The agent is correct that the option exists. He is not correct that it remains enforceable — a right of first refusal requires that the seller offer the item to the option-holder before completing a sale; Ali completed the sale before the agent could exercise the option.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — determine the timing: the option letter's exercise window required the agent to be notified within the same day. Ali completed the sale; the option was not invoked at the time of sale; it is now moot.*

*On success:* The agent accepts the timing argument, noting it for dispute with Ali rather than with you.

*On fail:* He files a road-court protest and delays you one day while a local magistrate reviews the timing. Magistrate sides with you.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Historian Who Heard the Phrase" (Tabriz)**

Ahmad al-Farghani has seen the Shah-Nameh's bracelet passage many times. He has never seen the armory inventory entry. He reads Entry 47 slowly.

"'Kept in memory of the one given away.'" He is quiet. Then: "The armory clerk was not writing a poem. He was writing an inventory. He wrote 'in memory' because that was the accurate description of the function of the bracelet — it served no combat purpose and no decorative purpose; its only purpose was memorial. The clerk documented the grief because grief was the function." He pauses. "Seven hundred years ago."

He adds a note beside Entry 47 in Arabic: *"The grief was already in the record before any scholar looked for it. A. al-Farghani, Tabriz, 1367."*

*Skill check: History DC 12 — recognize the significance of the armory clerk's precision: he used the language of inventory function to describe an emotional reality. This is what makes it primary evidence rather than secondary commentary.*

*On success:* Your recognition enables al-Farghani to articulate the argument precisely. His note becomes more specific: "The clerk was performing his function — describing what the object did. What it did was remember."

*On fail:* Al-Farghani adds his note but without the sharpened articulation.

*TOKEN check Act III — al-Farghani's note added to the folio margin.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Collector's Personal Offer" (road from Tabriz north)**

The Venetian collector himself is traveling north for other business. He is the person who purchased the earlier folios from Ali's inventory. He recognizes the leather sleeve immediately and understands what folio it must contain.

He makes a personal offer. He is knowledgeable, sincere, and generous. He has provided a good home for the previous folios. His case for his own collection is not weak.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — decline an offer that deserves a decent answer: the folio has been specifically designated for an institution that receives documents of grief and ambiguity, not documents of heroic achievement. The collector's collection is the wrong category.*

*On success:* He considers this for a moment. "The wrong category," he says slowly. Then: "Yes. All right." He asks to be told what Sweelinck says about the entry when you return, if you are willing.

*On fail:* He makes one more offer at the next junction. Still no threat — he is a collector, not a thug.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Bracelet's Other Register" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads Folio 47 and al-Farghani's marginal note. He reads Entry 47's phrase three times.

"'Kept in memory of the one given away.' The armory clerk was noting the object's function. The function of this bracelet was to remember another bracelet." He sets the folio down. "Seven hundred years ago, an armory officer in Sístán understood that grief can be the function of an object — not incidentally, but precisely, as the primary and only use. He documented it correctly. That is an act of institutional honesty that most institutions are not capable of."

He writes in the master ledger: *Grief Objects — Documented Loss Made Material; the armory inventory that describes grief as function. First entry. Weapons Chronicle Folio 47, Sístán, 7th century AD.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The bracelet kept because the other one was gone; the armory clerk who documented grief as a function of an object without being asked to; the archive creates Grief Objects — Documented Loss Made Material, for the seven-hundred-year-old entry that was honest before honesty of that kind had a category."**

---

## §KYA-11 — Vignette Cycle 11: "The Tree at the Border"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 9 documented what almost happened. Cycle 11 documents what could not be prevented. Afrásiyáb ordered that Saiáwush's blood must not touch the earth. A few drops fell anyway. A tree grew. The poem calls it the Saiáwush tree and says it has wondrous virtues. A 14th-century botanist in Samarkand documented the tree as a real botanical specimen — unusual bark, unusual leaf, local healing reports — without deciding whether the origin was supernatural.

### TOKEN: "The Botanist's Manuscript"

Eight folios by Farhad al-Samarqandi (died 1361), Samarkand, on "the Saiáwush tree of the Oxus border region." He describes the tree's location, appearance (unusual bark pattern, iridescent blue-green leaf undersides in autumn), and medical properties reported by local communities. Final paragraph: *"Local oral tradition attributes this tree to blood that fell on the ground despite an order that no blood should touch it. Whether this account is accurate, I cannot confirm. The tree is unusual."*

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "botanists_manuscript"
- `name`: "The Botanist's Manuscript"
- `description`: "Eight folios by Farhad al-Samarqandi (d. 1361) on the Saiáwush tree of the Oxus border region. Unusual bark, iridescent leaf undersides, reported healing properties. Final note: 'Whether the local account is accurate, I cannot confirm. The tree is unusual.' The botanist documented the tree without deciding its origin."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `CON` — Constantinople Byzantine Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SAM` (Samarkand — Yusuf al-Samarqandi, the botanist's son) → `CON` (Constantinople — Byzantine botanist Nikolaos Khrysaphes) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Botanist's Library" (Samarkand)**

Farhad al-Samarqandi died six years ago. His son Yusuf is a trader, not a scholar. He has been dispersing the library — selling texts to whoever is interested — and has not looked carefully at the Saiáwush manuscript because botanical texts do not appeal to his buyers.

He sets it on the table without ceremony. "It was his. He was particular about it. I don't know why."

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 11 — convince Yusuf that his father was particular about it for a reason worth honoring: a document that documents without deciding has specific value that other botanical texts do not.*

*On success:* Yusuf asks you to read him the final paragraph. You read: "Whether the local account is accurate, I cannot confirm. The tree is unusual." He is quiet for a moment. "He wrote that about a lot of things. He was that kind of man." He names a fair price.

*On fail:* He is uninterested in the manuscript's philosophical value and names a price based on the number of folios. Pay the commercial price.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Botanist's Manuscript received.*

---

**ACT II — "The Timurid Road Census" (Silk Road north of Samarkand)**

Timurid advance agents conducting a road census of materials leaving Samarkand. They are collecting botanical texts for a Timurid medical encyclopedia being compiled for Timur's court physicians.

The manuscript's plain leather cover has no title visible. The census agents identify it by heft and binding as a likely scholarly medical text.

*Skill check: Deception DC 12 — claim the manuscript is a personal letter collection from a family correspondence, not a botanical treatise. The cover's lack of title supports this framing.*

*On success:* The agents accept the framing and pass you through.

*On fail:* They open the manuscript, identify the botanical content, and place it on the Timurid requisition list. You must argue for a scholarly exemption: botanical texts on local flora are not medical texts, and the Timurid medical encyclopedia concerns therapeutics, not natural history. Persuasion DC 12 at the senior agent's review.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Byzantine Botanist's Observation" (Constantinople)**

Nikolaos Khrysaphes is a Byzantine botanist who has been collecting Anatolian natural history materials for fifteen years. He has a particular interest in trees mentioned in Eastern literary traditions.

He reads Farhad's description of the Saiáwush tree: unusual bark pattern, iridescent blue-green leaf undersides in autumn. He goes quiet.

"I have seen this tree. On the Oxus border. In 1358, on a survey trip." He produces his own notes from a cedar box — twelve years old, Oxus border region, one entry with the same bark description. "I didn't know what it was called. I wrote 'unusual.' He wrote 'unusual.' We saw the same tree."

*Skill check: History or Nature DC 12 — ask Khrysaphes the right question: not what the tree is, but what his independent observation means for the document's archival status.*

*On success:* He writes a notation on the manuscript's first folio: *"Confirmed: tree matching description seen at Oxus border, 1358. N. Khrysaphes, Constantinople, 1367. Independent observation. The tree exists regardless of its origin."*

*On fail:* He is willing to confirm but does not articulate the implication. He writes only: *"Tree confirmed seen, Oxus border, 1358."*

*TOKEN check Act III — Khrysaphes's notation added.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Natural History Collector's Claim" (road from Constantinople north)**

A Venetian natural history collector's agent on the northern road. He represents a patron who has been collecting Eastern botanical manuscripts and has a specific interest in trees mentioned in Persian literary traditions. He has a commercial letter authorizing purchase of botanical manuscripts from the Samarkand region.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — the commercial letter covers unrestricted export of botanical manuscripts from the Samarkand region; it does not cover seizure from a designated carrier who has already received the document in commission.*

*On success:* He accepts the legal argument and withdraws.

*On fail:* He claims the letter's language covers all unrestricted botanical manuscripts, including those already in transit. You must appeal to a Dominican legal review house on the road. Two-day delay; Dominican reviewer sides with you.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Record of the Border" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the manuscript and Khrysaphes's notation.

"Farhad al-Samarqandi says: 'Whether the local account is accurate, I cannot confirm. The tree is unusual.' Nikolaos Khrysaphes, in 1358, writes: 'Unusual.' Two observers, fourteen years apart, independent of each other, describing the same tree without deciding its origin." He sets the manuscript down. "The archive receives both the origin claim and the botanical evidence. They are not the same document. They are filed together because they describe the same tree."

He writes in the master ledger: *Physical Evidence of Legendary Events — botanical documentation; documents that neither confirm nor deny the origin but preserve what is physically there. First entry. The Saiáwush tree of the Oxus border region.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The blood fell despite the order; the tree grew where it was not supposed to grow; the botanist documented the tree without deciding whether the origin was possible; a second observer fourteen years later wrote 'unusual' independently; the archive files both descriptions with the origin claim and does not merge them."**

## §KYA-12 — Vignette Cycle 12: "The Portrait Gallery"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 11 documented a tree that grew from what the king tried to prevent. Cycle 12 documents what the prince built before he was destroyed. Saiáwush, exiled in Túrán, covered his palace walls with portraits of every great hero of Persia and Túrán — both sides. His son Kai-khosráu recognized Gíw from these portraits years later. The palace was razed after Saiáwush's murder. A list of the portraits survives in a court document from Khoten: the evidence of what the palace contained when it was a palace of peace.

### TOKEN: "The Portrait List"

Six folios of a Khoten court administrative record (12th-century copy from a possibly earlier original), listing Saiáwush's palace portrait gallery: names, descriptions, positions on the walls. Entry for the Rustem portrait: "east wall, third panel, larger than life, armed, with Rakush." Entry for Afrásiyáb: "west wall, first panel, on horseback, facing east." The list includes both Persian and Túránian heroes without comment. Bound in a Silk Road plain binding, water-stained on one corner.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "portrait_list"
- `name`: "The Portrait List"
- `description`: "Six folios listing Saiáwush's palace portrait gallery at Khoten. Both Persian and Túránian heroes, without comment. Rustem on the east wall; Afrásiyáb on the west wall, facing east. The palace was razed. The list survived. Kai-khosráu recognized Gíw from these portraits years later."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `NIS` — Nishapur Madrasa Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SAM` (Samarkand — the document arrives via the Silk Road from Khoten) → `NIS` (Nishapur — Iranian scholarly community, the document requires Persian historical context) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Document That Arrived from the East" (Samarkand)**

A Silk Road merchant named Rashid al-Samarqandi has received the Portrait List as partial payment for a debt — a Khotanese trader owed him money and paid in documents. The trader described it as "old court records from Khoten." Rashid cannot read the script (a hybrid Chinese-Persian administrative hand) and does not know what it is.

A translator in the Samarkand scholarly quarter has told him it is a palace portrait list from "a prince who built peace between Persia and Túrán." Rashid is now interested but does not know what to do with it.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince Rashid that the document has significant value and that its value is best preserved in a specific European archive rather than the Samarkand commercial manuscript market, where it will be purchased by someone who also cannot read it.*

*On success:* He names a fair price — neither generous nor exploitative. He adds: "Tell me what the translator said it was about, if you know."

*On fail:* He has already sent word to two manuscript dealers that the document is available. You must outbid the dealers' pending offers before they arrive.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Portrait List received, Silk Road plain binding.*

---

**ACT II — "The Chagatai Tax Official" (road from Samarkand toward Nishapur)**

A Chagatai administrative tax official is conducting a road inventory of commercial manuscript materials moving through his district. He taxes commercial manuscripts at five percent of declared value.

The Portrait List is a court administrative record — tax-exempt as a historical document — but the official is not certain of the category and wants clarification.

*Skill check: History DC 12 — articulate the distinction between a commercial manuscript (produced for sale) and a court administrative record (produced for administration): a list of a prince's palace portraits is an administrative inventory, not a commercial product.*

*On success:* He writes "court administrative record — exempt" on the travel paperwork.

*On fail:* He taxes it at five percent of declared value. Pay the tax or dispute it with his superior — a two-day delay.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Persian Scholar's Reading" (Nishapur)**

The Iranian scholarly community at Nishapur has a specialist in pre-Islamic Persian court records: Mansur ibn Farhad, an expert in Sogdian and early Persian administrative documents. He reads the Portrait List carefully.

The document raises an immediate scholarly problem: the Túránian heroes and Persian heroes are listed without distinction — they share wall space without any indication that Saiáwush considered them different. This is politically important. A Khorasani court tradition has maintained that Saiáwush was naive rather than principled — he served Afrásiyáb because he had no choice. The Portrait List implies intention.

A colleague of Mansur's wants to add a commentary note to the document arguing for the naive interpretation. The commentary would be secondary, but its presence in the document would shape how later readers approached it.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 13 — argue that the document's value is precisely its uninterpreted state: the list records what was on the walls without characterizing what it meant; adding an interpretive note would change the document's character from evidence to argued position.*

*On success:* Mansur agrees and tells his colleague directly: "The list is evidence. Leave it as evidence." He adds his own authentication note, specifically: "No interpretive commentary. The list speaks for itself."

*On fail:* The colleague's commentary is added as a marginal note. It is secondary and unauthorized; Sweelinck will note this.

*TOKEN check Act III — Mansur's authentication added; commentary issue noted.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Memory of a Palace" (road from Nishapur north)**

No formal obstacle on this road — instead, the weight of what you are carrying. You read the Portrait List at a caravanserai one evening.

Rustem on the east wall, facing west toward Afrásiyáb's portrait on the west wall, facing east. In the middle, presumably, the great hall where both nations could be at peace when both walls were in view. Kai-khosráu, Saiáwush's son, grew up with the portraits. When he finally escaped to Persia, he recognized Gíw — a warrior he had never met — from a portrait on the wall of a palace that had since been razed by the man who killed his father.

*No skill check.* This act is a narrative moment. The document passes through this night unchanged. The portrait gallery existed. The archive will hold its contents.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative; document intact.*

---

**ACT V — "The Inventory of a Palace of Peace" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the Portrait List carefully. He reads the entry for Rustem and the entry for Afrásiyáb.

"East wall and west wall, facing each other. Both Persian and Túránian, without comment, without distinction. A prince who built a palace with both armies on his walls was not naive — he was making an argument with paint." He is quiet. "The palace was razed by the man who eventually ordered his death. The argument was also razed. This list is the only evidence that the argument was made."

He writes in the master ledger: *Shah-Nameh Supplementary Records — Destroyed Locations; the inventory of Saiáwush's palace portrait gallery as evidence of a palace that was also a proposal. The palace was razed; the proposal was not destroyed while this list exists.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The prince who put both armies on his walls without comment made an argument with paint; the palace was razed by the man who had him killed; the list is the only evidence the argument was made; the archive files it under Destroyed Locations because the proposal was not destroyed while the list exists."**

## §KYA-13 — Vignette Cycle 13: "The King from the Sky"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 12 documented a palace built as an argument — both armies on the walls without comment. The palace was razed; the list survived. Cycle 13 documents an event that the Persian tradition turned into a folly: Kai-Káús built a flying throne, flew toward heaven, crashed. The Persian tradition called it hubris. A Chinese court received the crashed king, fed him for forty days, and returned him to Persia through a diplomatic mission. That court recorded the event in Han administrative format: a Persian king descended from the sky, was sustained as a guest, and was returned. The Chinese did not know they were describing a legend. They were describing an event.

### TOKEN: "The Chinese Court Record"

Three folios of a Han-format administrative document with parallel Persian glosses added by the Nestorian translator Dawud ibn Yusuf al-Nasrani, Tabriz, circa 1340. The original records the event as: "A Persian king descended with eagle-harnesses still attached, four eagles grounded, one throne overturned; sustained as guest forty days; returned by diplomatic mission to the western frontier." Dawud's Persian gloss beside the first line: "Kai-Káús. The shame was real. The return was also real."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "chinese_court_record"
- `name`: "The Chinese Court Record"
- `description`: "Three folios of a Han administrative record of Kai-Káús's eagle-throne crash: 'A Persian king descended from the sky, sustained forty days, returned by diplomatic mission.' The Chinese did not know they were describing a legend. Nestorian gloss: 'Kai-Káús. The shame was real. The return was also real.'"
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `CON` — Constantinople Byzantine archive quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`TAB` (Tabriz — Nestorian translator Dawud ibn Yusuf al-Nasrani) → `CON` (Constantinople — Nikephoros Katakalon) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Translator's Twenty-Year Problem" (Tabriz)**

Dawud ibn Yusuf al-Nasrani is a Nestorian translator who has worked in Tabriz for thirty years. Twenty years ago he received a Chinese court document as payment for a translation job — a Mongol official was liquidating a document cache and offered it in lieu of coin. Dawud translated it and recognized what it was.

He has not sold it. The Muzaffarid court traces its cultural legitimacy through Persian literary tradition, and a document that records the great Kai-Káús arriving from a crash with eagle-harnesses still attached is not what any Persian sultan wants circulating. Dawud does not want to be the man who released it.

He has heard of the Weimar archive. He knows it is European. He knows it has no political connection to any Persian court.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince Dawud that a document in a German archive will not return to Persia.*

*On success:* He lifts the document from a locked cedar box and sets it on the table. "No Persian sultan will ever see what is in a German archive." He names a fair price.

*On fail:* He wants a sworn declaration — written and witnessed — that the document will not be copied for any Muslim court or Ottoman official.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Chinese Court Record, Han paper, Persian glosses in margin.*

---

**ACT II — "The Venetian Courier's Question" (road from Tabriz toward Constantinople)**

A Venetian diplomatic courier named Lorenzo Soranzo recognizes Dawud's mark on the outer wrapper — he has handled sensitive translations from the same source before. He stops your horse on the road and asks what you are carrying.

*Skill check: Deception DC 12 — claim the document is a personal letter from Dawud to a colleague in Constantinople.*

*On success:* He accepts this and moves on without inspecting the document.

*On fail:* He takes a copy of the first folio's first line before you can prevent it. The copy is incomplete — he does not read Han script and copies only the shapes. It is useless but will generate curiosity at the Venetian embassy in Constantinople.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Outside View" (Constantinople)**

Nikephoros Katakalon has two other Han administrative documents in the Byzantine archive — trade records, not literary material. He recognizes the format immediately when you set the folios on his table. He reads Dawud's Persian gloss slowly.

"The Chinese described Kai-Káús without embarrassment. They had no reason for embarrassment. He was a foreign king who crashed in their territory and had to be fed and returned." He looks up. "The Persian tradition managed this event — folly of ambition, punishment of pride. The Chinese did not manage it. They recorded it."

He wants the document for his Byzantine-Persian relations section.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 13 — convince Katakalon that a document with no political motive is rarer than a document with context; Weimar preserves what no court archive will leave unmanaged.*

*On success:* He is quiet. "The outside view with no political motive is rarer still." He releases it. "Tell Sweelinck I want a copy of his filing note."

*On fail:* He makes a certified copy before releasing the original.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Storm on the Balkan Road" (road north from Constantinople)**

Rain. A Balkan mountain road in autumn. The document is in a waxed leather case but the road is flooding at the low river crossings.

*Skill check: Athletics DC 11 — ford the crossing without dropping the case.*

*On success:* The case arrives dry. The Chinese paper is intact.

*On fail:* The outer folio takes water damage at one edge. Sweelinck will note it.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Administrative Record of a Legend" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the three folios. He reads Dawud's gloss beside the first line.

"The Persians called this hubris. Kai-Káús flew toward heaven and fell. The lesson was about ambition. The Chinese recorded forty days of hospitality and a diplomatic return. The lesson was about logistics." He turns the third folio over. "The Chinese did not know they were describing a legend. They were describing a king who needed to be fed and returned. This is the only surviving document that records the event from outside the tradition that managed it."

He writes in the master ledger: *Foreign Administrative Records — Events Recorded Without Knowledge of Their Legendary Status; the Han administrative account of Kai-Káús's arrival, sustenance, and return, without knowledge that the event was being managed as a lesson about hubris by the tradition the king came from.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The Persian tradition called it hubris and managed the lesson; the Chinese called it forty days of hospitality and a diplomatic return; the document that survived the management is the one that did not know a legend was being told."**

---

## §KYA-14 — Vignette Cycle 14: "The Seeing Cup"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 13 documented an event that survived because a foreign court recorded it without knowing it was a legend. Cycle 14 documents an object that survived as a description. The Cup of Jemshíd showed its possessor all that transpired in the world. The cup was in the Ilkhanid treasury. In 1335 the Ilkhanid dynasty collapsed; the treasury was dispersed. The cup was melted for coin. A Mongol court clerk recorded what the cup was and what the court said it could do. That inventory entry survived because it was bundled with observatory documents and acquired by an astronomer who uses Ilkhanid administrative paper for provenance research.

### TOKEN: "The Jemshíd Inventory Entry"

A single folio from the Ilkhanid treasury dispersal, 1335. Mongol administrative hand with Persian translation below. Entry reads: "Vessel of green stone, diameter two spans, seven bands of inscription in ancient script, residue of former liquid visible at base; court notation: 'said to show the possessor all that transpires in the world.' Status: melted for coin in the fourth month." A later hand has added at the bottom: "The stone was green. The seven inscriptions have not been identified."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "jemshid_inventory_entry"
- `name`: "The Jemshíd Inventory Entry"
- `description`: "A single Ilkhanid treasury folio describing the Cup of Jemshíd before it was melted for coin in 1335. 'Said to show the possessor all that transpires in the world.' The cup is gone. The description of what the cup could do is what remains."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `MRG` — Maragha Observatory Scholar Quarter
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`MRG` (Maragha — astronomer Kamāl al-Dīn Ibrahim, document bundled with observatory paper) → `TAB` (Tabriz — manuscript dealer Hasan ibn Khidr al-Tabrizi) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Bundle from the Dispersal" (Maragha)**

Kamāl al-Dīn Ibrahim is an astronomer at the Maragha Observatory, second generation after the great Hulagu-era scholars. He uses Ilkhanid administrative documents for provenance research — the dispersal of 1335 scattered state documents widely, and some ended up mixed with observatory materials. The Jemshíd inventory entry arrived bundled with three folios of Ilkhanid astronomical grants. He has not looked at it closely.

He will sell the bundle but not individual folios.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 11 — explain that this specific folio is historically distinct from administrative grants; it is a description of a legendary object, and its scholarly value is separate from the provenance bundle.*

*On success:* He looks at the entry again. "If it is from a legend, the astronomers have no use for it." He sells it at the folio price.

*On fail:* He offers the whole bundle at bundle price; negotiate the specific folio out of the lot.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Jemshíd Inventory Entry, single folio, Mongol administrative hand.*

---

**ACT II — "The Treasury Provenance Mark" (road from Maragha toward Tabriz)**

An Ilkhanid customs holdover — a minor official who has served three dynasties and is not entirely certain who employs him anymore — recognizes the treasury dispersal provenance mark on the folio's outer edge. He believes this may be state property under Timurid rules covering Mongol administrative materials.

*Skill check: Deception DC 12 — argue that the dispersal was officially ordered; documents transferred during a legal dispersal passed into private hands legally; this folio is a private document now, not a state document.*

*On success:* He accepts the argument, visibly relieved not to have to report it up.

*On fail:* He holds the folio for a day while sending a message to the nearest Timurid administrative office asking for clarification.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Dealer's Prior Client" (Tabriz)**

Hasan ibn Khidr al-Tabrizi specializes in Ilkhanid material. He recognizes the treasury dispersal mark at once and tells you a prior client has standing interest in "all material related to Jemshíd."

*Skill check: History DC 12 — determine whether the document is verifiably the Jemshíd cup's record or an Ilkhanid object the court named after the cup: if the inventory entry describes a court item given the Jemshíd name internally, the dealer's client is buying a name and a description, not a provenance; this removes the prior-client standing.*

*On success:* The document is a record of what the Ilkhanid court claimed about an object it held; the court's claim is not the object's provenance. The prior-client obligation does not apply to an administrative notation.

*On fail:* He produces a letter from his client. You must outbid the pending offer.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "What the Cup Could Do" (road north from Tabriz)**

Evening camp. You look at the inventory entry. The cup showed Jemshíd all that transpired in the world. The cup was melted for coin in the fourth month of 1335. This folio is what remains.

The capacity to see the world is now what this page says the capacity was. The description has outlasted the cup. The green stone is gone. The seven inscriptions are gone. What the cup could do survives only in the sentence that said what it could do.

*No skill check.* The document passes through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative; folio intact.*

---

**ACT V — "The Described Power" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the inventory entry. He reads the notation. He reads the later hand's addition.

"The cup showed all that transpires in the world to the one who looked within. The cup was melted for coin. This entry is what remains." He sets the folio down. "The power survived as a description of a power. The object was destroyed; the record of what the object was said to do was not destroyed. Filed under Jemshíd Documents — Described Powers: the capacity exists here as a sentence, and the sentence is what the archive holds."

He writes in the master ledger: *Jemshíd Documents — Described Powers; the Ilkhanid treasury entry for the Cup of Jemshíd, including the court's notation of what the cup was said to do. The cup was melted. The notation survived.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The cup that could see the whole world was melted for coin; the sentence that said what the cup could do was not melted; the archive holds the capacity in the only form that survived the dispersal."**

---
