## §KYA-15 — Vignette Cycle 15: "The Ring in the Bird"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 14 documented a power that survived as a description after the object was destroyed. Cycle 15 documents an object whose function was not complete. Rustem was given a ring by his father Zál. He gave it to Sohráb before Sohráb died. The ring passed from Sohráb to a woman; from the woman to Barzú, Sohráb's son. When Rustem met Barzú in battle, Barzú showed the ring. Rustem recognized it and stopped killing. The ring appeared twice in Firdusi — Rustem to Sohráb, Sohráb dying — and the killing happened anyway. A Samarkand continuation text records the third appearance: the ring showed itself a third time and the killing stopped. The tradition needed the third appearance because a ring that stops killings had not yet stopped a killing.

### TOKEN: "The Barzú Continuation Folio"

Five folios from a Samarkand manuscript of the Barzú-Nameh — a continuation tradition not part of Firdusi's text. The folio records the ring episode: Sohráb gave the ring to a woman before his death; she gave it to Barzú; in battle, Barzú showed Rustem the ring; Rustem recognized it and stopped. The copying scribe added a marginal note: "The ring appeared to Rustem three times. Firdusi records two. This folio is the third."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "barzu_continuation_folio"
- `name`: "The Barzú Continuation Folio"
- `description`: "Five folios from a Samarkand continuation tradition (Barzú-Nameh): Sohráb's ring passed from woman to grandson Barzú; when Barzú showed Rustem the ring in battle, the killing stopped. Scribe's note: 'The ring appeared to Rustem three times. Firdusi records two. This folio is the third.'"
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SAM` (Samarkand — scholar Malik Shah al-Samarqandi selling off continuation texts) → `TAB` (Tabriz — Ahmad al-Farghani) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Scholar Selling Secondaries" (Samarkand)**

Malik Shah al-Samarqandi is a serious scholar of the Shah-Nameh funding the final stage of his commentary by selling off his collection of continuation texts — the Barzú-Nameh, the Sohráb expansions, the Farâmarz cycles. He considers them second-tier material.

He has the Barzú-Nameh in eleven gatherings. The ring episode is in gathering seven.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince him that the continuation tradition preserves something the main text does not: the ring's function was incomplete after Sohráb's death, and the tradition felt the incompleteness and added the third appearance.*

*On success:* He is briefly interested despite himself. "The ring appears to Rustem three times. A tradition that felt the ring needed to stop a killing understood the ring's function." He sells the five folios at a fair price.

*On fail:* He will sell only the complete gathering, not five individual folios; negotiate for gathering seven entire.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Barzú Continuation Folio, five folios, Samarkand hand.*

---

**ACT II — "The Timurid Road Census" (road from Samarkand toward Tabriz)**

A Timurid road census official is cataloguing literary manuscripts. The Shah-Nameh is registered literature. The Barzú-Nameh is a continuation text — not Firdusi, not registered, status ambiguous.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — read the official: does he care about the continuation text status, or is he doing rote paperwork and the ambiguity is his problem?*

*On success:* He cares about quantity, not literary status. Five folios at commercial rate; stamp and done.

*On fail:* He wants a scholarly authorization letter establishing the Barzú continuation as legitimate literature before he will register it without a hold.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "Ahmad al-Farghani's Note" (Tabriz)**

Ahmad al-Farghani is interested immediately in the ring's lifecycle. He wants to add a marginal note tracing the chain: Zál to Rustem, Rustem to Sohráb, Sohráb to the woman, woman to Barzú, Barzú to Rustem's recognition. He begins drafting it.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince al-Farghani that his ring-chain analysis belongs in his own scholarly publication, not in the document; if the document carries the ring's story, it should carry it without an interpretation layered in the margin.*

*On success:* He withdraws his pen. "The document tells the ring's story. My note would tell my reading of it." He adds only an authentication line and date.

*On fail:* He adds a brief marginal note on the last folio tracing the ring's three appearances. It is careful scholarship but it is now in the document.

*TOKEN check Act III — al-Farghani's authentication added.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Collector at the Mountain Pass" (road from Tabriz north)**

A collector — Ibrahim al-Qazwini — specializes in identification objects from Persian legend. He wants the Barzú folio for completeness. His hired riders are waiting at the mountain pass junction.

*Skill check: Athletics DC 12 — keep moving through the junction without being stopped.*

*On success:* You pass through. The riders do not pursue beyond the junction.

*On fail:* Altercation at the junction; the folio is briefly seized but recovered. Minor damage to the outer gathering cover.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Third Appearance" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the Barzú folio carefully. He reads the scribe's note.

"The ring appeared to Rustem twice in Firdusi. Both times the ring was associated with Sohráb — given to Sohráb, found on Sohráb after the killing. The killing happened anyway. The tradition needed the ring to work. A ring that stops killings must stop a killing." He is quiet. "The Barzú continuation gave the ring a third appearance and the killing stopped. The function was complete. Filed under Identification Objects — Objects That Stopped Killing: the ring appeared three times; only the third time did the killing stop; the archive holds the third appearance."

He writes in the master ledger: *Identification Objects — Objects That Stopped Killing; the Barzú continuation tradition's record of the ring's third appearance, in which the ring's function was finally fulfilled. Firdusi records two appearances. This folio is the third.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The ring appeared to Rustem twice and the killing happened anyway; the continuation tradition gave the ring a third appearance because a ring that stops killings had not yet stopped a killing; the archive holds the third appearance."**

---

## §KYA-16 — Vignette Cycle 16: "The Warriors' Mounds"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 15 documented a ring whose function was finally completed in a continuation tradition. Cycle 16 documents a location that may or may not be real. At the end of his reign, Kai-khosráu heard a divine voice telling him his time was complete. He warned his warriors not to follow him into the mountains. Five followed anyway — Gíw, Tus, Gudarz, Fariburz, Bizhan. They were buried in a snowstorm. The Shah-Nameh describes the mounds. In 1289, a merchant traveling the Caspian foothills found five stone mounds of unusual regularity. His local guide said: "The warriors who followed the king who walked away." The merchant wrote an account. He noted the mounds were real. He refused to confirm the identification.

### TOKEN: "Yusuf al-Jilani's Account"

Two folios by Yusuf al-Jilani, merchant and occasional traveler, dated 1289. He describes the Caspian foothills road, the five mounds, the unusual regularity, the local guide's comment. A sketch map on the second folio shows the mounds' positions. Final line: "Whether the local account is accurate, I cannot confirm. The mounds are there."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "jilani_mounds_account"
- `name`: "Yusuf al-Jilani's Account"
- `description`: "Two folios from 1289: a traveler found five stone mounds of unusual regularity in the Caspian foothills. Local guide: 'The warriors who followed the king who walked away.' Sketch map included. Final line: 'Whether the local account is accurate, I cannot confirm. The mounds are there.'"
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**New node required:**
- `TBZ` — Trebizond Black Sea Port (Byzantine-controlled city on the Black Sea coast; major transit point for travelers from the Caspian and eastern Anatolia; distinct from TAB = Tabriz)

**Existing nodes used:**
- `CON` — Constantinople Byzantine archive quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`TBZ` (Trebizond — Armenian merchant Grigor Khatchaturyan, acquired from Yusuf al-Jilani's estate) → `CON` (Constantinople — Nikephoros Katakalon) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Estate of a Cautious Traveler" (Trebizond)**

Armenian merchant Grigor Khatchaturyan acquired Yusuf al-Jilani's travel account as part of an estate settlement — al-Jilani died in 1301 and his effects passed through several hands before Grigor bought a document bundle at auction in Trebizond. He knows it is a travel account from the Caspian foothills and that it mentions mounds. He does not know what the mounds are.

*Skill check: History DC 11 — identify the specific terrain description in Yusuf's account as consistent with the Shah-Nameh's description of the warriors' resting place, and explain to Grigor what the mounds might be.*

*On success:* He is surprised. "A merchant who walked past a legend and wrote 'I cannot confirm.'" He sells it, pleased to have it in better hands than an estate lot.

*On fail:* He sells it as a curiosity travel account without understanding the significance.

*TOKEN granted Act I — Yusuf al-Jilani's Account, two folios, sketch map intact.*

---

**ACT II — "The Gate on the Bosphorus" (road from Trebizond toward Constantinople)**

A Byzantine toll gate on the Bosphorus crossing. The gate official reviews travel documents. The sketch map in the second folio looks like a military survey of the Caspian foothills — five positions marked, compass bearings noted.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — explain that five stone mounds in the Caspian foothills are not military positions; they are landscape features marked by a merchant for personal reference.*

*On success:* He stamps it through, slightly embarrassed.

*On fail:* He confiscates the sketch page pending cartographic review. Retrieve it at the Constantinople administrative office — one day's delay.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "Katakalon's Reading" (Constantinople)**

Nikephoros Katakalon looks at the sketch map and the folio text carefully. He reads the local guide's comment and Yusuf's final line.

"He confirmed the mounds are there. He refused the identification. This is unusual — most travelers either confirm or ignore; very few refuse explicitly while noting the physical evidence." He is genuinely interested.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — determine whether Katakalon wants the document as evidence of a real location (scholarly value) or as a curiosity of a traveler's unusual epistemic caution (collector's value). The distinction shapes Sweelinck's categorization.*

*On success:* He wants it as evidence. "The archive should hold both facts equally — the mounds exist, and the question exists." He releases it without copying.

*On fail:* He treats it as a curiosity and makes a copy for his file on unusual travel accounts.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Venetian Comparandist" (road north from Constantinople)**

A Venetian traveler named Marco da Feltre has been to the Caspian foothills region and has his own notes from 1351. He wants to compare his notes with Yusuf's sketch map.

*Skill check: Investigation DC 12 — determine whether his interest is genuine scholarly comparison or a pretext to establish a prior literary claim to the location's identification.*

*On success:* His account is genuine but less precise — his sketch is freehand, not measured. He gives you a copy of his own brief notes as supplementary documentation. "Two accounts is better than one."

*On fail:* His sketch is a post hoc copy of a published route description; he is establishing a prior claim. His supplementary documentation is designed to dilute the Jilani account's uniqueness.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Open Question" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads Yusuf al-Jilani's account. He reads the local guide's comment. He reads the final line.

"Kai-khosráu told the warriors not to follow him. They followed. They were buried. Five mounds." He taps the sketch map. "Five mounds in the Caspian foothills, unusual regularity, 1289. The guide says one thing. The traveler confirms the mounds and refuses to confirm the guide." He is quiet. "The question is correctly preserved. Filed under Physical Evidence of Legendary Events — Unconfirmed Location Records: the mounds are real; the identification is the question; the archive leaves it open with him."

He writes in the master ledger: *Physical Evidence of Legendary Events — Unconfirmed Location Records; Yusuf al-Jilani's 1289 account of five stone mounds matching the Shah-Nameh description of the warriors' resting place. The traveler confirmed the mounds exist and refused to confirm the identification. The archive preserves both facts.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The warriors followed the king who walked away and were buried by a snowstorm; five mounds are in the Caspian foothills; the traveler confirmed the mounds and refused to confirm the identification; the archive preserves both facts and leaves the question open with him."**

## §KYA-17 — Vignette Cycle 17: "The Golden Basin"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 16 preserved an open question about a physical location. Cycle 17 documents an instrument that recognized its own function. When Kai-khosráu captured Afrásiyáb after decades of war, the execution was performed in the same manner as Saiáwush's murder: throat cut over a golden basin. Gersíwaz, who had arranged Saiáwush's death, was executed the same way. A Mongol court clerk inventorying the Samarkand treasury recorded: "Used in the Saiáwush ceremony and the Afrásiyáb ceremony." The clerk was performing his function: describing what the object did. What the object did was remember.

### TOKEN: "The Samarkand Treasury Page"

A single folio from a Samarkand Mongol court treasury inventory, 13th century. Entry: "One golden basin, diameter three spans, depth one span, channel around the inner rim, no ornament; notation: 'used in the Saiáwush ceremony and the Afrásiyáb ceremony'; provenance: Persian royal treasury, acquired Ilkhanid period." A later hand has added: "The channel was for the blood." Status line: "Melted for coin in the fourth month of the year of the rat."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "samarkand_treasury_page"
- `name`: "The Samarkand Treasury Page"
- `description`: "Single treasury folio: 'One golden basin, channel around inner rim. Used in the Saiáwush ceremony and the Afrásiyáb ceremony.' Later note: 'The channel was for the blood.' Status: melted for coin. The clerk recorded what the object did. What it did was remember."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `MRG` — Maragha Observatory Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SAM` (Samarkand — document dealer Yaqub al-Khwarazmi) → `MRG` (Maragha — scholar Mahmud al-Arzani, complementary melting receipt) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Dealer's Large Lot" (Samarkand)**

Yaqub al-Khwarazmi deals in Mongol administrative documents — post-Ilkhanid dispersal materials, treasury inventories, court records. The Samarkand treasury page is part of a large lot from an official's estate. He knows the basin entry is unusual — the notation about two ceremonies marks it as something other than a routine treasury item — but he has not worked out why.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — explain the significance of the dual notation: Kai-khosráu chose the same instrument both times; the notation records that the court understood it was the same instrument and the same ceremony; this is a record of deliberate historical symmetry.*

*On success:* He is moved by the explanation. "The clerk recorded what the object did. What it did was perform the same ceremony twice." He names a price reflecting the significance.

*On fail:* He wants a scholar's letter confirming historical significance before adjusting his price.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Samarkand Treasury Page, single folio, Mongol administrative hand.*

---

**ACT II — "The Timurid Seal Problem" (road from Samarkand toward Maragha)**

A Timurid military unit on the road. The officer recognizes the Mongol court seal on the folio's provenance block and stops you. Under Timurid regulations, Mongol administrative materials with active provenance seals may be state property subject to review.

*Skill check: History DC 12 — establish that the basin was melted for coin, as the status line records; an object that has been destroyed cannot be claimed as state property; the folio is a historical record of a former treasury item, not a current property document.*

*On success:* The officer checks the status line himself, sees "melted for coin," and releases you. "A melted basin is not a property claim."

*On fail:* Detained one day while the officer sends a courier to the nearest Timurid administrative office for confirmation.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Complementary Record" (Maragha)**

Scholar Mahmud al-Arzani studies Mongol administrative records at Maragha. He has a complementary document: a receipt for the melting of treasury items in the relevant year, which includes the basin. He wants to keep both documents together — the treasury entry and the melting receipt form a single administrative sequence.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 13 — argue that the documents serve different scholarly functions: the melting receipt is administrative closure; the treasury entry with the dual-ceremony notation is historical evidence; separating them clarifies both; a copy of the melting receipt can accompany the original.*

*On success:* He sees the distinction. "The basin was melted. The function was recorded. These are different facts." He gives you a copy of the melting receipt. "Let the archive have both facts."

*On fail:* He insists on keeping both originals together; you keep the treasury page but only a copy of the melting receipt.

*TOKEN check Act III — copy of melting receipt added.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Flooded Road" (road from Maragha north)**

Rain. A flooded road section before the northern junction. The treasury page is in a sealed case, but the ford is deep.

*Skill check: Athletics DC 11 — cross the flooded ford without losing the sealed case.*

*On success:* The folio arrives dry and intact.

*On fail:* The outer edge of the folio takes water damage. Sweelinck notes it.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Instrument That Remembered" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the treasury entry. He reads the later hand's addition.

"The channel was for the blood. The court clerk noted the function: used in the Saiáwush ceremony and the Afrásiyáb ceremony." He is quiet. "Kai-khosráu chose the same instrument both times. The clerk recorded the choice. The notation is the court's acknowledgment that the second ceremony was the same as the first." He sets the folio down. "The basin recognized its function. The clerk recognized the basin's function. The basin was then melted. The notation was not melted."

He writes in the master ledger: *Instruments of Historical Symmetry — the Samarkand treasury record of the golden basin used in both the Saiáwush execution ceremony and the Afrásiyáb execution ceremony. The court notation records that the court knew it was the same instrument and the same ceremony. The basin was melted; the notation survived.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"Kai-khosráu used the same basin twice; the court noted 'the Saiáwush ceremony and the Afrásiyáb ceremony'; the clerk was recording what the object did; what the object did was remember; the basin was melted; the notation was not."**

---

## §KYA-18 — Vignette Cycle 18: "Zerdusht's Tree"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 17 documented an instrument that remembered its own function. Cycle 18 documents a tree that was cut down by the wrong authority. Zoroaster planted a cypress before King Gushtásp's fire-temple at Kashmar. The tree grew for centuries. In 861 CE the Abbasid Caliph al-Mutawakkil ordered it felled despite protest from the entire province. A traveler described its dimensions as the last wagons left: forty for the branches, three hundred for the trunk. A Yazd Zoroastrian community has kept that description for five hundred years because it is the only surviving measurement of what was lost.

### TOKEN: "The Kashmar Description"

Three folios by Mahmud ibn Dawud al-Yazdi, 10th-century Zoroastrian scholar, written shortly after the felling. Describes the tree's dimensions, the planting tradition (attributed to Zoroaster personally, one branch for each year of Gushtásp's conversion), and the removal: "The branches required forty wagons to carry away. The trunk required three hundred. The road from Kashmar to Baghdad was blocked for eleven days." Final line: "There will not be another tree of this kind because there is no longer a reason to plant one."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "kashmar_description"
- `name`: "The Kashmar Description"
- `description`: "Three folios describing the Zoroastrian cypress of Kashmar before and during its felling in 861 CE. Forty wagons for the branches; three hundred for the trunk. Final line: 'There will not be another tree of this kind because there is no longer a reason to plant one.'"
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `YAZ` — Yazd Zoroastrian Fire Temple Quarter
- `MRG` — Maragha Observatory Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`YAZ` (Yazd — fire-keeper community, five-century custodianship of the description) → `MRG` (Maragha — astronomer and botanist Nasir al-Din Tusi's student, interested in ancient tree records) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "Five Centuries of Custody" (Yazd)**

Fire-keeper Firouz ibn Ardeshir at the Yazd temple. The Kashmar Description has been in the community for five hundred years. No one has tried to take it. No one outside the community knows it exists. Firouz is old enough to remember when the waqf dispute nearly destroyed the temple's other records.

He does not want to give it up. He wants to know why a European archive is better than a Zoroastrian community.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 11 — argue that a community under legal pressure is a fragile custodian; an archive with no political position in Persian legal disputes is a more stable home; the community keeps its knowledge of the tree even if the paper goes elsewhere.*

*On success:* He is quiet for a long time. "The tree is gone. The description of the tree is not the tree. You may take the description." He gives you a sealed leather case.

*On fail:* He wants a sworn assurance that the document will be accessible to Zoroastrian scholars who travel to Weimar. He will wait for the assurance in writing.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Kashmar Description, three folios, sealed leather case.*

---

**ACT II — "The Botanical Checkpoint" (road from Yazd toward Maragha)**

A Muzaffarid agricultural inspector on the main road — his job is to track the movement of agricultural goods, seeds, plant materials. He has been told to watch for Zoroastrian sacred-site materials being moved out of the region. He stops you.

*Skill check: History DC 12 — identify the document as a 10th-century botanical and historical record of a tree that no longer exists; there is no living tree to be protected; the document describes a historical event, not a current agricultural matter.*

*On success:* He accepts this. "A tree that no longer exists is not within my mandate." He stamps the case through.

*On fail:* He holds it two days pending verification that the document is historical, not current. A waqf court clerk has to confirm the Kashmar cypress was felled in 861 CE.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Astronomer's Botanical Interest" (Maragha)**

Ibn Rashid al-Maraghi is a student of Nasir al-Din Tusi's school and has maintained a collection of records on trees mentioned in pre-Islamic Persian literature. He has heard rumors of the Kashmar Description and is delighted to see it.

He wants to copy it and add it to his botanical archive. He also wants to add a note connecting the cypress dimensions to a known species — he believes the tree was a variety of Cupressus sempervirens with an unusual growth pattern.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince him that his species identification note, if added to the document, will become the primary way future readers encounter it; the document should carry Mahmud ibn Dawud al-Yazdi's dimensions and Yazdi's final sentence, not a botanical correction.*

*On success:* He withdraws his note. "The dimensions are what matter. My identification belongs in my own collection." He makes a clean copy for his records and certifies the original without annotation.

*On fail:* He adds a two-line species note to the outer folio. It is accurate scholarship. Sweelinck will note it is secondary.

*TOKEN check Act III — Ibn Rashid's authentication added.*

---

**ACT IV — "Forty Wagons" (road north from Maragha)**

Evening. Camp on the road north. You read the final passage: forty wagons for the branches, three hundred for the trunk, the road from Kashmar to Baghdad blocked for eleven days.

The tree was planted by Zoroaster. The Abbasid Caliph had it cut down. The cutting took eleven days of road-blocking. Someone counted the wagons.

*No skill check.* The document passes through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative.*

---

**ACT V — "The Description of a Tree That Cannot Be Planted Again" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the three folios. He reads the dimensions. He reads the final line.

"There will not be another tree of this kind because there is no longer a reason to plant one." He sets the folio down. "The cypress was planted at a conversion. The conversion is five centuries finished. The tree grew through five centuries. The Caliph had it removed. Forty wagons for the branches; three hundred for the trunk. The road was blocked eleven days. Someone stood there and counted." He is quiet. "Filed under Sacred Trees — Descriptions Surviving Destruction: the dimensions and the occasion of the Kashmar cypress, in the hand of a scholar who understood that the reason to plant this kind of tree no longer existed."

He writes in the master ledger: *Sacred Trees — Descriptions Surviving Destruction; Mahmud ibn Dawud al-Yazdi's 10th-century description of the Kashmar cypress: dimensions, felling, and final line. The tree was planted at a conversion. The description survives the tree by five centuries.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The tree was planted at a conversion; the tree grew five centuries; the Caliph had it removed; someone counted forty wagons for the branches and three hundred for the trunk; the scholar who described it understood that there was no longer a reason to plant another one."**

## §KYA-19 — Vignette Cycle 19: "Maníjeh's Letter"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 18 documented a tree whose description survived its destruction. Cycle 19 documents a letter whose form survived its occasion. Maníjeh was a princess stripped of her royal clothes and left barefoot near her imprisoned lover's pit, so she could carry him food without being recognized as royalty. She composed a letter describing her condition. The letter was not a petition — she had no court to petition. It was a statement of captivity in the only language she still had. Five hundred years later, a Nishapur scribal workshop adapted the letter-form for women filing legal petitions of captivity in the Islamic courts: the form of a statement written from a position of nothing survived because it was the most accurate form available.

### TOKEN: "The Petition Adaptation"

Four folios from a Nishapur scribal workshop, dated 1247. A formal adaptation of the Maníjeh letter-form for use in women's legal petitions, with scribal notes explaining each adapted element. The standard opening reads: "I write from confinement without name or title, because those were taken from me before this letter could be written." Marginal note by the workshop master: "Adapted from the letter attributed to Maníjeh daughter of Afrásiyáb. Used as model for fourteen confirmed petitions in the Nishapur court record between 1203 and 1247."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "petition_adaptation"
- `name`: "The Petition Adaptation"
- `description`: "Four folios: a Nishapur scribal adaptation of Maníjeh's letter-form for legal petitions of captivity. Opening: 'I write from confinement without name or title, because those were taken from me before this letter could be written.' Used as model for fourteen confirmed petitions. The form survived the occasion."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `NIS` — Nishapur Madrasa Scholar Quarter
- `RGS` — Ragusa Genoese merchant quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`NIS` (Nishapur — scholar Aisha bint Khalid al-Nishapuri, keeper of the workshop archive) → `RGS` (Ragusa — Genoese dealer Benedetto Grimaldi, Islamic legal manuscripts) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Workshop Archive" (Nishapur)**

Aisha bint Khalid al-Nishapuri runs what remains of her family's scribal workshop — her grandfather established it, her father maintained it, she has maintained it through thirty years of successive disruptions. The Petition Adaptation is in the workshop archive, still in use: she has produced two petition copies from it in the last decade.

She is not certain the document belongs in a European archive. It is still functional here.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — convince her that a document in active use can be copied for use while the original is preserved; the form of the letter is available to copy; the provenance chain — that this adaptation was made in 1247 and used for fourteen specific petitions — is in the original, not in copies.*

*On success:* She makes a clean working copy for the workshop's continued use and hands you the original. "The form is what matters. The form is in any copy. The chain of use is only here."

*On fail:* She wants the copy made first, in her presence, before releasing the original. One day's work.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Petition Adaptation, four folios, workshop hand.*

---

**ACT II — "The Ottoman Letter Checkpoint" (road from Nishapur toward the west)**

An Ottoman military-administrative official checking documents moving west. A letter in a woman's voice claiming to write from confinement without name or title is exactly the kind of document that could be a current political communication — a captive noblewoman's petition to a foreign court.

*Skill check: Deception DC 11 — establish that the document is a scribal model text attributed to a legendary figure, not a current correspondence; the workshop notes in the margin make clear it is a template, not a sent letter.*

*On success:* He reads the marginal note: "attributed to Maníjeh daughter of Afrásiyáb." He is satisfied. "The daughter of a legendary king is not currently captive."

*On fail:* He holds it overnight while he sends a rider to confirm there is no current Ottoman noblewoman named Maníjeh filing petitions.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Genoese Dealer's Market" (Ragusa)**

Benedetto Grimaldi deals in Islamic legal manuscripts — contracts, court records, procedural texts — for the Florence and Genoa legal scholarly markets. He recognizes the Petition Adaptation as a legal document immediately and values it as a procedural text.

He has a buyer in Florence who collects Islamic legal procedure. The Petition Adaptation would fit the collection perfectly and would be well-preserved.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — determine whether the Florence buyer's collection is a serious scholarly archive (in which case the document would be as well-preserved as at Weimar) or a gentleman's curiosity cabinet (in which case the document's provenance chain would be lost in a private collection).*

*On success:* The Florence buyer is a gentleman collector, not a scholar. His collection is private and inaccessible. The Weimar archive is the correct destination for a document whose value includes public scholarly access.

*On fail:* Benedetto argues the Florence collection is comparable to Weimar; you must argue the specific value of the workshop provenance chain to justify the Weimar preference.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Form of a Statement Written from Nothing" (road from Ragusa north)**

Evening. You read the opening line again. "I write from confinement without name or title, because those were taken from me before this letter could be written."

The form was invented by a woman who had nothing left to write from except the fact of her condition. The form was reused fourteen times by women who also had nothing left to write from except the fact of their condition. The form is accurate because the condition it describes is always the same condition.

*No skill check.* The document passes through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative.*

---

**ACT V — "The Form That Outlasted Its Occasion" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the four folios carefully. He reads the opening line. He reads the marginal note about the fourteen petitions.

"The form was invented by a woman who had nothing. The form was borrowed by women who also had nothing. The form works because the condition it describes is precise: confinement, without name, without title, those having been taken first." He is quiet. "The occasion is five hundred years past. The form was still in use in 1247 and possibly after. Filed under Women's Documents — Forms That Survived Their Occasions: the scribal adaptation of Maníjeh's letter-form, with the chain of use intact, because the form is accurate to a condition that does not change."

He writes in the master ledger: *Women's Documents — Forms That Survived Their Occasions; the Nishapur scribal adaptation of the Maníjeh letter-form for legal petitions of captivity. Fourteen documented uses between 1203 and 1247. The form is accurate to the condition. The condition recurs.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The form was invented by a woman who had nothing left except the fact of her condition; the form was reused by women who also had nothing left; the form survives because the condition it describes is precise and the condition recurs."**

---

## §KYA-20 — Vignette Cycle 20: "The Dragon-Cart Design"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 19 documented a form invented from a position of nothing. Cycle 20 documents a design invented to solve a specific problem. Isfendiyár needed to pass through a forest guarded by a dragon. A wainwright guild received the commission: build a cart that kills at speed. The design problem was precise — blades must project at an angle that clears the wheel rotation. The guild solved it. The cart was built. The dragon was killed. A Tabriz metalworkers' guild archive holds the commission specification, including the blade-angle diagram and the guild note on the central engineering challenge. The solution is in the design; the design is in the archive.

### TOKEN: "The Blade-Cart Commission"

A rolled parchment from the Tabriz metalworkers' guild archive, 11th-century copy of the original commission specification for Isfendiyár's blade-cart. Includes a cross-section diagram showing blade angle, hub attachment, and wheel clearance calculation. Guild note beside the diagram: "The design problem: blades at angle below 38° foul the wheel at speed. Blades at angle above 52° lose cutting force. Solution: jointed bracket at axle hub, allowing blade angle adjustment between 40° and 50° during travel." Commission header: "For Isfendiyár, prince. Purpose: forest passage. Delivery: seven days."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "blade_cart_commission"
- `name`: "The Blade-Cart Commission"
- `description`: "Rolled parchment: the wainwright commission for Isfendiyár's blade-cart. Cross-section diagram showing blade angle and wheel-clearance calculation. Guild note: 'Blades below 38° foul the wheel. Above 52° they lose force. Solution: jointed bracket at axle hub.' The problem was precise. The solution is in the design."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `TAB` — Tabriz Jalayirid Scholar Quarter
- `NIS` — Nishapur Madrasa Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`TAB` (Tabriz — retired metalworker Sulayman ibn Khalid al-Tabrizi, heir to dissolved guild archive) → `NIS` (Nishapur — mechanical scholar Mahmud ibn Yahya al-Jazari) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Dissolved Guild" (Tabriz)**

Sulayman ibn Khalid al-Tabrizi is the last member of a metalworkers' guild that dissolved twenty years ago when the guild-master died without heirs. Sulayman inherited the archive. He is a retired craftsman, not a scholar, and does not understand the value of what he has kept.

He knows the blade-cart commission is unusual — the diagram is unlike any standard wagon design — but he does not know it is from a legend.

*Skill check: History DC 12 — recognize the commission's connection to the Isfendiyár legend and explain to Sulayman what the design document represents: the solution to a legendary engineering problem, preserved in a craftsman's archive because craftsmen preserve solutions.*

*On success:* He is proud without quite understanding why. "The guild solved it. Seven days delivery." He names a modest price.

*On fail:* He has already offered the archive to a Tabriz brass merchant who wants the parchment as scrap. You must act before the merchant's agent arrives.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Blade-Cart Commission, rolled parchment, guild hand.*

---

**ACT II — "The Military Engineer's Interest" (road from Tabriz toward Nishapur)**

A Timurid military engineer named Ahmad Beg on the road east recognizes the diagram format — he designs siege equipment for the Timurid army. He stops you. The blade-cart design is not classified anywhere, but it represents a functional weapons design. He wants to evaluate it.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — argue that the commission is for a specific legendary vehicle, not a general weapon design; the blade-angle solution is specialized for a cart that must kill while traveling through a forest, which has no current military application; the document's value is historical, not tactical.*

*On success:* He examines the diagram. "Jointed bracket at axle hub. Clever, but the wheel track is too narrow for field use." He releases it.

*On fail:* He takes a copy of the diagram and files a note with the Timurid engineering office.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Mechanical Scholar's Question" (Nishapur)**

Mahmud ibn Yahya al-Jazari is a mechanical scholar in the tradition of al-Jazari's famous Book of Knowledge of Ingenious Mechanical Devices. He is immediately fascinated by the blade-cart diagram. He wants to understand whether the jointed-bracket solution was original to this commission or borrowed from an earlier design tradition.

He begins an investigation that will take months. He wants to keep the document for the duration of the investigation.

*Skill check: Investigation DC 12 — determine whether the jointed-bracket mechanism appears in any other document in al-Jazari's collection or related mechanical treatises, which would establish whether it was independent invention or transmission.*

*On success:* You find a partial parallel in a 9th-century Persian mechanical treatise — the bracket principle existed, but the blade-angle application is original. This resolves al-Jazari's question without requiring the document to stay. He authenticates it and releases it with a note.

*On fail:* He insists the investigation requires the original document. Negotiate a three-week timeline with a deposit.

*TOKEN check Act III — al-Jazari's authentication added.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Solution in the Design" (road from Nishapur north)**

Evening. You unroll the commission and look at the diagram again. Blade angle between 40° and 50°. Jointed bracket at the axle hub. Seven days delivery.

A guild received a commission to build a vehicle that kills at speed. The guild identified the engineering constraint — the wheel-fouling problem at angles below 38°. The guild solved it. The dragon died. The solution is still in the diagram.

*No skill check.* The document passes through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative.*

---

**ACT V — "The Craftsman's Record of a Solved Problem" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck examines the diagram carefully. He reads the guild note.

"The problem was stated precisely. The solution was stated precisely. The delivery date was met." He looks at the commission header: 'For Isfendiyár, prince. Purpose: forest passage. Delivery: seven days.' "The guild did not record whether the dragon was killed. The guild recorded that the commission was completed. The commission was for a cart that kills at speed. Whether the specific intended death occurred is outside the document's scope." He is quiet. "Filed under Military Engineering Records — Legendary Commissions: the blade-cart design, commission to completion, with the engineering solution preserved in the diagram. The guild solved the problem. The archive holds the solution."

He writes in the master ledger: *Military Engineering Records — Legendary Commissions; the Tabriz metalworkers' guild commission specification for Isfendiyár's blade-cart, including the jointed-bracket wheel-clearance solution. The guild's record ends at delivery. Whether the commission's purpose was achieved is not within the document's scope.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The commission was precise; the engineering problem was stated and solved; the guild delivered in seven days; whether the dragon was killed is outside the document's scope; the archive holds the solution to the wheel-fouling problem."**

## §KYA-21 — Vignette Cycle 21: "The Kazú-Tree Arrow"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 20 documented a design solution preserved in a craftsman's commission. Cycle 21 documents a material specification preserved in a forester's tag. The Símúrgh told Rustem exactly what he needed to kill Isfendiyár: a forked branch from a Kázú tree, east-facing, grown at a river junction, taken at dawn. Rustem was reluctant — killing Isfendiyár was also killing what was best about Isfendiyár. He took the branch. He made the arrow. The Símúrgh was specific because the specificity was the point: only this branch, from this tree, at this location, could do what needed to be done. A Sístán forester documented the tree in 1289 because the tree matching the specification still existed, and he wanted the chain of custody to be clear.

### TOKEN: "The Forested Branch"

A preserved forked branch of Kázú-wood, approximately 60 cm, sealed in a cedar case with a bronze latch. Tied to the branch by a cord is a documentation tag in the hand of forester Farhad al-Sistani, 1289: "Kázú-wood. East-facing growth. Three-generation tree. River junction at the northern Dasht-e Kavir approach. Branch taken at dawn per the Símúrgh's specification as recorded in the Shah-Nameh of Firdusi. Chain: one branch, one tag, one case. Kept by the Sistani forester family from this date."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "forested_branch"
- `name`: "The Forested Branch"
- `description`: "A preserved forked Kázú-wood branch in a cedar case. Documentation tag: 'East-facing tree, three generations, river junction. Taken at dawn per Símúrgh specification. Chain: one branch, one tag, one case.' The Símúrgh was specific. The forester documented the specificity."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SIS` — Sístán Province Road Town
- `NIS` — Nishapur Madrasa Scholar Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SIS` (Sístán — forester family, fourth-generation custodianship since 1289) → `NIS` (Nishapur — botanist-scholar Zayd ibn Ibrahim al-Nishapuri) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Fourth Generation" (Sístán)**

Jamshid al-Sistani is the fourth-generation keeper of the branch and its documentation tag. His great-great-grandfather Farhad al-Sistani took the branch in 1289. The family has kept it in the cedar case. They have not opened it since Farhad made the tag.

Jamshid knows the story but not whether the tree still exists. He has been considering whether a forester's family is the right long-term custodian for a document-and-specimen that has now outlasted the original purpose of preservation.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 11 — convince Jamshid that a dedicated archive can preserve the branch and the tag together without separating the physical specimen from its documentation chain, which is the thing his family has protected.*

*On success:* He unlocks the cedar case and confirms the branch and tag are intact. "The chain is what matters. The chain is: one branch, one tag, one case. Keep them together." He hands over the case.

*On fail:* He wants a written guarantee that the branch and tag will be archived together, never separated. He will wait for the guarantee before releasing it.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Forested Branch, cedar case, branch and documentation tag.*

---

**ACT II — "The Botanical Customs Question" (road from Sístán toward Nishapur)**

A road inspector at a permanent checkpoint is cataloguing botanical materials for an agricultural survey. Plant materials require identification and destination certification.

*Skill check: Deception DC 11 — claim the branch is a botanical specimen being transported to a scholar in Nishapur for classification, which is effectively true.*

*On success:* He stamps it through as "dry botanical specimen — scholarly transport."

*On fail:* He wants a scholar's receiving letter from Nishapur before allowing the specimen through. One-day delay while a rider carries the request.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Botanist's Identification" (Nishapur)**

Zayd ibn Ibrahim al-Nishapuri is a botanist-scholar who has worked on identifying plants from Persian classical literature. He is deeply interested in the Kázú branch — Kázú-wood is mentioned only once in the Shah-Nameh and has not been identified as a species.

He opens the cedar case, examines the branch with great care, and makes observations: grain pattern consistent with a Prunus variety, unusual resin density, iridescent sheen on the inner wood visible only in cross-section.

*Skill check: History DC 12 — identify from the Firdusi text what the Símúrgh specified about the tree's location characteristics, and match those characteristics against Farhad's tag to confirm the documented tree is the specified tree, not a different tree Farhad mislabeled.*

*On success:* The tag's east-facing orientation and river junction specification match the Shah-Nameh text precisely. Zayd confirms: "The documentation is internally consistent with the source specification. Whether the tree was the tree is the question that cannot be resolved further." He adds his botanical notes to the case's exterior and authenticates the tag.

*On fail:* One specification in the tag does not clearly match the text. Zayd is uncertain. He adds a note flagging the ambiguity.

*TOKEN check Act III — Zayd's botanical authentication added to case exterior.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Weight of a Specification" (road from Nishapur north)**

Evening. You hold the cedar case. The branch inside is 700 years old. The tag says "taken at dawn per Símúrgh specification." The Símúrgh was specific because the specificity was the point: only this tree, this branch, this orientation, at this junction, at dawn.

Rustem took the branch reluctantly. He knew what it meant to take it. The arrow that killed Isfendiyár was made from a branch that could only be taken reluctantly because the Símúrgh had been specific about what kind of killing this was.

*No skill check.* The document and specimen pass through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative.*

---

**ACT V — "The Specification and the Reluctance" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck opens the cedar case carefully. He reads the documentation tag. He looks at the branch without touching it.

"The Símúrgh specified the tree precisely. The forester documented the tree precisely. The specification required a specific act of reluctance — only this branch, from this tree, which is to say: not any branch, which is to say: there was a cost to the specificity." He closes the case. "The branch is the specification and the reluctance together. Filed under Legendary Material Specifications — Objects Documented at Origin: the Kázú-wood branch with its unbroken chain of documentation from 1289 to the present; the Símúrgh's specification preserved in the forester's tag; the reluctance preserved in the fact that Rustem took the branch anyway."

He writes in the master ledger: *Legendary Material Specifications — Objects Documented at Origin; the Kázú-wood branch with documentation tag by Farhad al-Sistani, 1289, matching the Símúrgh's specification in the Shah-Nameh. The chain is intact: one branch, one tag, one case.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The Símúrgh specified the tree, the orientation, the junction, the dawn; the forester documented the tree that matched; the branch and the tag have been kept together since 1289; the archive holds the specification and the reluctance in the same case."**

## §KYA-22 — Vignette Cycle 22: "The Pit Record"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 21 documented a specification that required reluctance. Cycle 22 documents a commission that required no reluctance at all. Shughad, Rustem's treacherous half-brother, commissioned seven pits on a hunting road. Each pit was filled with sword-blades. He then arranged for the King of Kábul to invite Rustem hunting. Rustem fell into the pits and died. A builder recorded the commission in professional notes: dimensions, blade specifications, eleven days, three workers, payment received. The builder recorded what he built without recording what it was for. Or he knew what it was for. The document does not say.

### TOKEN: "The Builder's Account"

Five folios from the Herat library, preserved since at least the 11th century. Builder's professional notes on the seven-pit commission: each pit four cubits deep, two wide; blade density three per handspan; blades hardened iron, upward-angled at forty-five degrees; total construction time eleven days; three workers; cost itemized. Final entry: "Commissioned by Shughad. Seven pits. Road at the Kábul hunt preserve. Payment received in full." No further notation.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "builders_account"
- `name`: "The Builder's Account"
- `description`: "Five folios: professional builder's notes on the seven-pit commission. Dimensions, blade specifications, eleven days, three workers. Final entry: 'Commissioned by Shughad. Seven pits. Payment received in full.' The builder recorded what he built. Whether he knew what it was for is outside the document's scope."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**New node required:**
- `HRT` — Herat Library and Scholar Quarter (major city in the Kart dynasty; significant manuscript library; under increasing Timurid pressure in 1367; future Timurid capital after conquest)

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`HRT` (Herat — Herat library scholar Yusuf ibn Dawud al-Harawi) → `SAM` (Samarkand — Timurid legal scholar Mahmud ibn Ali al-Samarqandi) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Library Under Pressure" (Herat)**

Yusuf ibn Dawud al-Harawi is a senior scholar at the Herat library under the Kart dynasty. The Timurid expansion is making Herat's future as an independent scholarly center uncertain. Yusuf has been quietly identifying the most irreplaceable documents in the library for possible relocation.

The Builder's Account is one of them — not because anyone at the Herat library fully understands it, but because it is unique and very old and clearly connected to something important.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — explain the significance of the Builder's Account (the professional record of Rustem's death-trap, preserved without moral commentary) and convince Yusuf that a stable European archive is the right destination for it before the library's future becomes uncertain.*

*On success:* He is grateful for the clarity. "I have been wondering where to send it. A German archive is not within anyone's sphere of military interest." He wraps the folios himself.

*On fail:* He wants the Herat library to receive credit in the archive's catalogue — a formal acknowledgment that the document came from Herat. He will wait for a written commitment.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Builder's Account, five folios, Herat library seal on outer wrapper.*

---

**ACT II — "The Timurid Inspection" (road from Herat toward Samarkand)**

A Timurid officer on the road is conducting an inspection of materials leaving the Herat library. There is no formal prohibition on library documents leaving, but the Timurid court is interested in what the Herat library holds.

*Skill check: Insight DC 12 — read the officer: is he following general orders to catalogue library outflows (routine), or does he have specific intelligence about valuable documents being moved?*

*On success:* He is following general orders. He catalogues the document as "builder's professional notes — historical, non-political." Cleared.

*On fail:* He has specific intelligence. He knows a valuable document is leaving the library and wants to know more before clearing it. Persuade him that builder's notes on pit construction have no current strategic value.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "The Legal Scholar's Argument" (Samarkand)**

Mahmud ibn Ali al-Samarqandi is a legal scholar with a specialty in historical property liability. He has heard of the Builder's Account and believes it has legal significance: the seven pits were built on the Kábul hunt preserve; the builder is named indirectly through Shughad's commission; the document might support a claim of contractor liability in historical criminal acts.

*Skill check: Investigation DC 12 — determine whether the Builder's Account contains enough identifying information to constitute a legal liability document under 14th-century Khorasani contract law, or whether it is simply a professional record that cannot be used as evidence because the parties are long dead and the jurisdiction no longer exists.*

*On success:* The jurisdiction no longer exists. The parties are dead. The document is historical evidence of a completed commission, not actionable testimony. The legal argument fails on procedural grounds before it reaches substance.

*On fail:* The legal scholar argues there is precedent for using historical documents to establish patterns of liability in current land-ownership disputes. He wants to keep it for the argument. Negotiate him down to a copy.

*TOKEN check Act III.*

---

**ACT IV — "Payment Received in Full" (road from Samarkand north)**

Evening camp. You read the final entry again: Commissioned by Shughad. Seven pits. Payment received in full.

The builder recorded the payer, the quantity, and the fact of payment. He did not record what the pits were for. He may not have known that the pits were for the greatest hero of Persia. He may have known. He recorded what a professional records: the work, the client, the payment.

Rustem died in the pits. The builder had been paid before Rustem arrived at the hunt preserve.

*No skill check.* The document passes through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative.*

---

**ACT V — "The Professional Record Without Moral Commentary" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck reads the five folios carefully. He reads the final entry.

"Seven pits. Payment received in full." He is quiet for a long time. "The builder recorded what he built. The commission was from Shughad. Shughad knew what the pits were for. The builder may have known. The document does not say." He sets the folios down. "A professional record that does not include the consequences of the work is a document of a specific kind — the work is complete when the payment is received; what happens after is outside the scope of the commission. Filed under Instruments of Death — Builder's Records Without Moral Commentary: the professional account of the construction that killed Rustem; the builder's record ends at payment; the rest is outside the document's scope."

He writes in the master ledger: *Instruments of Death — Builder's Records Without Moral Commentary; the builder's professional notes for Shughad's seven-pit commission. Dimensions, blade specifications, construction time, cost, and final entry: payment received in full. Whether the builder knew what the pits were for is not recorded. The archive preserves the question with the document.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The builder recorded the dimensions, the blades, the eleven days, the three workers, and that payment was received in full; whether he knew what the pits were for is not recorded; the professional record ends at payment; the rest was outside the scope of the commission."**

## §KYA-23 — Vignette Cycle 23: "The Four Goblets"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 22 documented a professional record that ends at payment without recording the consequences. Cycle 23 documents an object whose provenance claim cannot be confirmed or denied. Alexander went to Dárá's court disguised as his own envoy. He sat at the table and took four golden goblets. A Samarkand Mongol court inventory records one goblet with a twin-fish motif and a provenance notation: "One of four goblets. Dárá's table. Taken by Alexander." The goblet passed to private hands at the Samarkand dispersal. The notation is intact. Whether the goblet is one of the four is the question the archive cannot answer.

### TOKEN: "The Twin-Fish Goblet"

A gold goblet, palm-sized, with two fish in relief around the bowl in Achaemenid style. Stamped on the base: a Samarkand Mongol court inventory mark, dated. Inside the base, scratched in a later hand: "One of four. Dárá's table. Taken by Alexander. Three others recorded in Mongol treasury. This one Samarkand dispersal." The fish face each other at the rim. The gold is real. The rest is the notation.

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "twin_fish_goblet"
- `name`: "The Twin-Fish Goblet"
- `description`: "A gold goblet, two fish in Achaemenid relief. Inventory notation: 'One of four. Dárá's table. Taken by Alexander.' The other three are in Mongol treasury records. This one passed to private hands at the Samarkand dispersal. The goblet is real. Whether it is one of the four is the question."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `SAM` — Samarkand Scholar and Merchant Quarter
- `CON` — Constantinople Byzantine archive quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`SAM` (Samarkand — antiques dealer Bakr ibn Umar al-Samarqandi) → `CON` (Constantinople — Nikephoros Katakalon) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Dispersal Lot" (Samarkand)**

Bakr ibn Umar al-Samarqandi deals in objects from the Mongol court dispersal — seals, cups, jewelry, administrative items. He has the Twin-Fish Goblet with its inventory mark and the scratched notation inside. He knows it is from the Mongol court inventory. He does not know whether the provenance claim is plausible or a later addition by someone who wanted to enhance the goblet's value.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — argue that a provenance claim that cannot be confirmed or denied has scholarly value precisely as an unconfirmed claim; the archive is the right place for an object whose value is the question it carries, not a settled answer.*

*On success:* He is persuaded by the framing. "An object that carries a question no one can answer is unusual inventory." He names a fair price.

*On fail:* He wants a written appraisal from a scholar confirming the Achaemenid-style analysis before adjusting the price.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Twin-Fish Goblet, gold, Mongol inventory mark.*

---

**ACT II — "The Luxury Tax Checkpoint" (road from Samarkand toward Constantinople)**

A Timurid checkpoint for taxable luxury goods on the western road. Gold objects are taxable at fifteen percent of declared value. The goblet is clearly gold.

*Skill check: Deception DC 12 — claim the goblet is being transported as a scholarly artefact for archival deposit, not for sale or personal use; scholarly transport of historical artefacts is exempt from the luxury tax under the standard scholarly exemption.*

*On success:* He checks the exemption register. Scholarly transport is listed. "The fish look old." He stamps it through.

*On fail:* He taxes it at fifteen percent of declared value. Pay or request an exemption hearing — two-day delay.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "Katakalon's Hellenistic Source" (Constantinople)**

Nikephoros Katakalon has a Hellenistic-era fragment mentioning "four cups taken from the table of Dárá" in the context of Alexander's intelligence mission. He is excited. He wants the goblet for the Byzantine archive to sit alongside the textual fragment — the object and the source together.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 13 — convince Katakalon that Weimar is better positioned to hold the object alongside the full Shah-Nameh cycle; Constantinople has the Hellenistic fragment but not the Persian narrative tradition; Weimar can hold the object within the complete literary context that gives it meaning.*

*On success:* He is reluctant but follows the argument. "The full context is in Weimar now. The goblet belongs with the context." He provides a copy of his Hellenistic fragment for Sweelinck's file.

*On fail:* He makes a detailed sketch of the fish motif and the inventory mark before releasing it.

*TOKEN check Act III — Katakalon's fragment copy added.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Object and the Question" (road north from Constantinople)**

A Venetian collector at the Constantinople gate offers to buy the goblet outright. He has bought three Achaemenid-style objects in the last year and wants to complete a set.

*Skill check: Athletics DC 11 — keep moving through the gate meeting without stopping for his negotiation.*

*On success:* You pass him without pausing. He calls after you but does not follow.

*On fail:* He blocks the gate briefly. You lose half a day to the standoff before he accepts you are not selling.

*TOKEN check Act IV.*

---

**ACT V — "The Goblet and the Question It Carries" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck holds the Twin-Fish Goblet carefully. He reads the scratched notation inside the base. He sets it down.

"The other three goblets are in the Mongol treasury records. This one is not — it passed out of the treasury at the Samarkand dispersal." He is quiet. "The notation may be accurate. It may be a later enhancement. The fish are Achaemenid-style and the gold is old. The inventory mark is genuine Mongol court." He picks it up again. "The archive cannot answer the question. The archive holds the object and the question together." He writes in the master ledger: *Objects of Contested Provenance — Claims That Cannot Be Verified or Denied; the Twin-Fish Goblet with Mongol inventory mark and notation claiming it as one of four goblets taken by Alexander from Dárá's table. The claim is intact; the verification is not possible; the archive holds both.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"Alexander took four goblets from Dárá's table while disguised as his own envoy; three are in the Mongol treasury records; this one carries a notation that it is one of the four; the archive cannot confirm or deny the claim; the archive holds the object and the question together."**

---

## §KYA-24 — Vignette Cycle 24: "The Wall Stone"

### Cycle vs. Cycle — What This Angle Reveals

Cycle 23 documented an object whose provenance claim is intact but unverifiable. Cycle 24 documents a building whose existence is attested and whose builder is named but whose current condition is unknown from within the archive. Alexander built a wall in the high mountain passes to contain Gog and Magog. The Shah-Nameh describes the wall in detail: its iron and copper construction, its gate, the inscription. A merchant trading beyond the Caucasus in 1341 found a wall in the high passes whose local people call Alexander's Wall. He took one stone. The stone has the eagle-and-star seal device that local people associate with Sikander. The wall still stands, the merchant noted. The archive will not be able to verify this either.

### TOKEN: "The Seal Stone"

A cut limestone block, 30 cm × 20 cm × 10 cm, with an eagle-spread-wings-and-star device carved into one face, and below the device four Greek letters: ΑΛΕΞ. The block's cut edges show it was removed from a larger course. The merchant Artashes Babikian attached a tag to the stone: "Found at Alexander's Wall, high Caucasus pass. The local people call it Sikander's Wall. I took one stone. The eagle is on most of the stones I looked at. The wall still stands."

**Token mechanics:**
- `id`: "seal_stone"
- `name`: "The Seal Stone"
- `description`: "A limestone block carved with an eagle-and-star device and the letters ΑΛΕΞ. Merchant's tag: 'Found at Alexander's Wall, high Caucasus pass. The eagle is on most of the stones I looked at. The wall still stands.' The wall is real. Whether it is Alexander's wall is the question."
- `grant_act`: 1
- `take_act`: 5
- `check_acts`: [2, 3, 4]

### Nodes

**Existing nodes used:**
- `TBZ` — Trebizond Black Sea Port
- `CON` — Constantinople Byzantine archive quarter
- `WM` — Weimar archive

### Route

`TBZ` (Trebizond — Armenian merchant Artashes Babikian, arrived from the Caucasus trading route) → `CON` (Constantinople — Nikephoros Katakalon, final appearance) → `WM` (Weimar archive)

### Five-Act Vignette Play

---

**ACT I — "The Stone from Beyond the Pass" (Trebizond)**

Artashes Babikian has just returned from a trading expedition beyond the Caucasus. He brought back the stone as a curiosity — he is a practical man and did not expect it to be worth money; he brought it because the eagle carving was striking and because the local people were proud of the wall and he wanted something to show his family.

He has not tried to sell it. He is mildly surprised anyone is interested.

*Skill check: History DC 11 — identify the ΑΛΕΞ letter sequence and eagle-and-star device as consistent with Macedonian royal seals as described in Byzantine historical sources, and explain to Artashes that the stone is possibly the only portable fragment of the legendary wall in circulation.*

*On success:* He is genuinely interested now. "I could have taken more stones. I took one." He names a modest price and gives you the tag he attached to the stone in transit.

*On fail:* He gives it away for the cost of the expedition inconvenience — he just wants to know it went somewhere appropriate.

*TOKEN granted Act I — The Seal Stone, limestone, merchant's tag attached.*

---

**ACT II — "The Cartographic Question" (road from Trebizond toward Constantinople)**

The Byzantine toll gate on the Bosphorus crossing again. A large carved stone block is unusual cargo. The gate official is also a part-time cartographer for the Byzantine military and is immediately interested in the eagle device — it looks like a boundary marker from a military installation.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — explain that the wall is in the high Caucasus passes, which is not Byzantine territory; this is a privately carried historical artefact from a trade route, not a boundary marker from any current military installation.*

*On success:* He accepts this. "The Caucasus passes are not ours." He stamps it through but makes a note of the ΑΛΕΞ letters for his own cartographic file.

*On fail:* He wants a Byzantine military-administration clearance before allowing the stone through — two days.

*TOKEN check Act II.*

---

**ACT III — "Katakalon's Final Word" (Constantinople)**

Nikephoros Katakalon's last appearance in the KYA cycle. He has now received or evaluated seven KYA documents. He looks at the Seal Stone for a long time.

"ΑΛΕΞ. Four letters. If there are more letters on the other stones, the wall spells something." He looks at Artashes's tag. "The wall still stands." He is quiet. "I have spent many years with documents about things that no longer exist. This is an object from something that still exists, described by a legend, and I cannot go there to verify it."

He does not want this one for himself. He recognizes that the question it carries is the archive's kind of question.

*Skill check: Persuasion DC 12 — this time, Katakalon is already inclined to release it; the check is whether you can persuade him to write a formal letter for Sweelinck attesting to the Byzantine documentary tradition about Alexander's Wall, which would establish the archive context for the stone.*

*On success:* He writes a short attestation: "Known in Byzantine sources as the Caspian Gate or Alexander's Barrier. The Shah-Nameh description is consistent with the Byzantine accounts. The wall's current state is reported as standing by independent testimony, 1341." He folds it and places it under the stone's merchant tag.

*On fail:* He declines to write the attestation, uncertain he should formally associate the Byzantine archive with an unverified physical claim.

*TOKEN check Act III — Katakalon's attestation added.*

---

**ACT IV — "The Wall Still Stands" (road north from Constantinople)**

Evening. The stone is heavy in the pack. The eagle carving faces up when you set the pack down. Four letters. A merchant wrote: the wall still stands.

Alexander built the wall to contain what was on the other side of the pass. The Shah-Nameh describes the wall. Artashes Babikian found a wall and took one stone and the eagle was on most of the stones he looked at and he did not take more because he was a practical man.

*No skill check.* The stone passes through this night unchanged.

*TOKEN check Act IV — narrative.*

---

**ACT V — "The Stone from a Wall That Is Still Standing" (Weimar)**

Archivus Sweelinck looks at the Seal Stone for a long time. He reads the merchant's tag. He reads Katakalon's attestation.

"The wall is described in the Shah-Nameh. The wall is described in Byzantine sources. A merchant found a wall in the high Caucasus passes in 1341 and the local people call it Alexander's Wall. He took one stone. The wall was still standing when he took it." He runs a finger along the cut edge. "ΑΛΕΞ. This is one stone of many. The archive holds one stone. The wall holds the rest." He writes in the master ledger: *Physical Evidence of Legendary Events — Objects Removed from Standing Structures; the Seal Stone from the Caucasus wall called Alexander's Wall by local people and consistent with Byzantine and Persian literary descriptions. The wall is reported as standing as of 1341. The archive holds one stone.*

*TOKEN taken Act V.*

---

### Theme

**"The wall is described in two literary traditions; a merchant found a wall matching the description; the eagle is on most of the stones; the wall still stands; the archive holds one stone; the wall holds the rest."**

---
